\displaystyle=(\ln a)(\ln b)^{2}F(x)(e^{2x\ln b}2\ln a\ln b+e^{x\ln b}\ln b)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+(\ln a)(\ln b)^{2}(e^{2x\ln b}\ln a+e^{x\ln b})\ln a\ln bF(x)e^{x\ln b}

\displaystyle=(\ln a)(\ln b)^{3}F(x)\left(2e^{2x\ln b}\ln a+e^{x\ln b}+e^{3x\ln b}(\ln a)^{2}+e^{2x\ln b}\ln a\right).

Notice

e^{x\ln b}\leq e^{\ln ae^{x\ln b}}-1<F(x),

therefore we have

\displaystyle F^{\prime\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\leq(\ln a)(\ln b)^{2}F(x)\left(F(x)^{2}\ln a+F(x)\right),

\displaystyle F^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\leq(\ln a)(\ln b)^{3}F(x)\left(F(x)^{3}(\ln a)^{2}+3F(x)^{2}\ln a+F(x)\right).

We thus can take

\displaystyle\rho_{1}(t)=(\ln a)(\ln b)^{2}t\left(t^{2}\ln a+t\right),

\displaystyle\rho_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=(\ln a)(\ln b)^{3}t(t^{3}(\ln a)^{2}+3t^{2}\ln a+t),

which are clearly non-negative and non-decreasing on
[0,\infty)
.

•

Next we highlight the natural example of any self-concordant function
F:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Thus

|F^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)|\leq 2F^{\prime\prime}(x)^{3/2}\leq 2|F^{\prime\prime}(x)|^{3/2}.

Suppose
F
satisfies
Assumption
1.1
. Then there exists a non-negative, non-decreasing
\rho_{1}
such that
|F^{\prime\prime}(x)|\leq\rho_{1}(F(x))
. Thus,

|F^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)|\leq 2\rho_{1}(F(x))^{3/2}.

Since
\rho_{1}
is non-negative and non-decreasing,
\rho_{2}(t):=2\rho_{1}(t)^{3/2}
is as well, and thus
Assumption
1.2
is satisfied.

Next, we show that the regularity assumptions Assumptions 1 and 3 of
Xie et al. (2024)
, which they need for their guarantees finding SOSPs, are less general than
Assumption
1.2
when
F
is twice-differentiable. To do so we show they imply
Assumption
1.2
, and are hence subsumed by
Assumption
1.2
.

When
F
is twice-differentiable, their Assumption 1 implies
(L_{0},L_{1})
-smoothness. As shown in
Proposition
A.2
, this means that

\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq\rho_{0}\left(F(\boldsymbol{w})\right)\text{ where }\rho_{0}(x)=2L_{0}^{1/2}x^{1/2}+\frac{5L_{1}^{2}}{L_{0}^{1/2}}x^{3/2}.

Their Assumption 3 implies for
M_{0},M_{1}\geq 0
and some
\delta>0
that for all
\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}
with
\left\|\boldsymbol{w}-\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}\right\|\leq\delta
,

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{w}-\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}\right\|\left(M_{0}+M_{1}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\right).

Combining this with the earlier display gives for all
\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}
with
\left\|\boldsymbol{w}-\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}\right\|\leq\delta
,

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{w}-\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}\right\|\left(M_{0}+M_{1}\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}))\right),

where
\rho_{0}(x)=2L_{0}^{1/2}x^{1/2}+\frac{5L_{1}^{2}}{L_{0}^{1/2}}x^{3/2}
.
We thus see that
F
satisfies
Assumption
1.2
with the non-decreasing, non-negative function
\rho_{2}(x)=M_{0}+M_{1}\left(2L_{0}^{1/2}x^{1/2}+\frac{5L_{1}^{2}}{L_{0}^{1/2}}x^{3/2}\right)
, where the latter two properties are evident as
\rho_{0}(\cdot)
is non-decreasing and non-negative.

A.3
Proofs of Technical Results

Now, we prove general results used throughout our work.
We prove
Corollary
1
, which gives us control over the gradient:

Proof of
Corollary
1
.

Applying Lemma 11,
De Sa et al. (2022)
with
\Phi
in place of
F
, we obtain

\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq\rho(F(\boldsymbol{w}))\sqrt{2\theta(F(\boldsymbol{w}))}=\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w})),

where
\theta(\cdot)
is defined as in the statement of
Corollary
1
. To prove
\rho_{0}(x)
is increasing, simply note
\theta
and thus
\sqrt{\theta}
are clearly increasing, and are both non-negative.
\rho_{1}
is non-decreasing and non-negative as well, thus
\rho_{0}
is non-decreasing and non-negative.
∎

We also prove the central
Lemma
3.1
, which is very important to our results: it lets us control the change in function value under our regularity assumptions. We first state the following Lemma from
Li et al. (2023a)
, a generalization of Gronwall’s Inequality:

Lemma A.5

(Lemma A.3,
Li et al. (2023a)
)
.

Let
\alpha:[a,b]\rightarrow[0,\infty)
and
\beta:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
be two continuous functions. Suppose
\alpha^{\prime}(t)\leq\beta(\alpha(t))
almost everywhere over
(a,b)
. Let
\phi(u)=\int_{0}^{u}\frac{1}{\beta(v)}\mathrm{d}v
. Then for all all
t\in[a,b]
,

\phi(\alpha(t))\leq\phi(\alpha(a))-a+t.

This allows us to prove
Lemma
3.1
, which is an extension of Lemma A.4,
Li et al. (2023a)
:

Proof of
Lemma
3.1
.

The proof is essentially identical to the proof of Lemma A.4,
Li et al. (2023a)
. Let
\boldsymbol{z}(t)=(1-t)\boldsymbol{x}+t\boldsymbol{y}
,
\alpha(t)=F(\boldsymbol{z}(t))
. Then for all
t\in(0,1)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\rightarrow t}\frac{\alpha(s)-\alpha(t)}{s-t}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{s\rightarrow t}\frac{\left\lvert F(\boldsymbol{z}(s))-F(\boldsymbol{z}(t))\right\rvert}{s-t}

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\lim_{s\rightarrow t}\frac{F(\boldsymbol{z}(s))-F(\boldsymbol{z}(t))}{s-t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}F(\boldsymbol{z}(t))\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\nabla F(\boldsymbol{z}(t))^{\top}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{z}(t)))\left\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}\right\|,

the last step using
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}))
. Let
\beta(x)=\left\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}\right\|\rho_{0}(x)
and let
\phi(u)=\int_{0}^{u}\frac{1}{\beta(v)}\mathrm{d}v
. Thus,
\alpha^{\prime}(t)\leq\beta(\alpha(t))
almost everywhere. Applying
Lemma
A.5
gives

\phi(F(\boldsymbol{y}))=\phi(\alpha(1))\leq\phi(\alpha(0))+1=\phi(F(\boldsymbol{x}))+1.

Let
\psi(u)=\left\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}\right\|\phi(u)=\int_{0}^{u}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(v)}\mathrm{d}v
, which is clearly strictly increasing. Consequently we obtain from the above and assumption on
\boldsymbol{y}
that

\displaystyle\psi(F(\boldsymbol{y}))

\displaystyle\leq\psi(F(\boldsymbol{x}))+\left\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\psi(F(\boldsymbol{x}))+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{x})+1)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{F(\boldsymbol{x})}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(v)}\mathrm{d}v+\int_{F(\boldsymbol{x})}^{F(\boldsymbol{x})+1}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(v)}\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{F(\boldsymbol{x})+1}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(v)}\mathrm{d}v=\psi(F(\boldsymbol{x})+1).

Since
\psi
is strictly increasing, taking inverses implies

F(\boldsymbol{y})\leq F(\boldsymbol{x})+1,

as desired.
∎

We also introduce the following Lemma, which lets us exploit
Assumption
1.2
to control the Lipschitz constant of the Hessian of
F
.

Lemma A.6
.

Suppose
F
satisfies
Assumption
1.2
. Suppose
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are such that
\left\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}\right\|\leq r
for some
r>0
. Then

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{y})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\right\|\cdot\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}}}\rho_{2}\left(F(\boldsymbol{u})\right).

In particular, we have

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{y})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\right\|\cdot\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{y},r)}\rho_{2}\left(F(\boldsymbol{u})\right).

Proof.
Consider
\delta>0
, either from
Assumption
1.2
if the second case of
Assumption
1.2
holds, and otherwise set to some arbitrary positive real. Similar to the proof of
Lemma
3.1
, divide the line segment between
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}
into
N=\frac{\left\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\right\|}{\delta}
equally spaced segments of length
\delta
between points
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}
, where we define
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}=\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{N-1},\boldsymbol{x}_{N}=\boldsymbol{y}
. Thus
\left\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\right\|=N\delta
.

Suppose for all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}}
we have
\left\|\nabla^{3}F(\boldsymbol{u})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq L
. Consider any
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}
in the line segment
\overline{\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}}
. Applying this for
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}+t(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})
for
t\in[0,1]
, which always lies in the line segment
\overline{\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}}
, we obtain

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle\nabla^{3}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}+t(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})),\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t\right\|\leq L\left\|\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right\|.

Consequently irrespective of which case of
Assumption
1.2
holds, because
\left\|\boldsymbol{x}_{i}-\boldsymbol{x}_{i-1}\right\|\leq\delta
, we have for each
i,1\leq i\leq N
that

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}_{i})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}_{i-1})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{x}_{i}-\boldsymbol{x}_{i-1}\right\|\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}}}\rho_{2}\left(F(\boldsymbol{u})\right).

Now Triangle Inequality gives

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{y})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}_{i})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}_{i-1})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\|\boldsymbol{x}_{i}-\boldsymbol{x}_{i-1}\right\|\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}}}\rho_{2}\left(F(\boldsymbol{u})\right)

\displaystyle\leq N\delta\cdot\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}}}\rho_{2}\left(F(\boldsymbol{u})\right)

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\right\|\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}}}\rho_{2}\left(F(\boldsymbol{u})\right),

as desired.
∎

We will also generalize the proof of
Theorem
3.1
to show that GD, when initialized in the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set
\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
with appropriate step size defined in terms of
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
, never increases function value.

Lemma A.7
.

Consider any
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and consider iterates
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of GD initialized at any
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set. If the step size
\eta
of GD is at most
\frac{1}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
where
L_{1}(\cdot)
is defined as per
(
3.2
)
, then
F(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\leq F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
for all
t\geq 0
.

Proof.
It suffices to prove this for
t=1
; a simple inductive argument then establishes this for all
t\geq 0
. We have
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\eta\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
.
By
Corollary
1
and because
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
,
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|\leq\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\leq\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))
. Thus by choice of
\eta
and definition of
L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\right\|=\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|\leq\eta\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}.

By
Lemma
3.2
, because
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, for all
\boldsymbol{p}
in the line segment
\overline{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}}
we have
\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{p})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
.
By
Lemma
A.1
, it follows that

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{u}_{1})

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}+\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta^{2}}{2}\cdot\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})+\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}\cdot\left(-\eta+\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta^{2}}{2}\right).

Noting
-\eta+\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta^{2}}{2}\leq 0
for
\eta\in\left[0,\frac{2}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\right]
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Framework

Proof of
Theorem
2.1
.

For convenience, for all
n\geq 0
, define
p_{n}:=1-n\cdot\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}}\delta(\boldsymbol{u})
.
Also let
T=\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}}\left\{\frac{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\Delta(\boldsymbol{u})}\right\}
.

Lemma B.1
.

For any
n\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{E}_{n}
be the event that the sequence of iterates
(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq n-1}
satisfies either:

1.

The event
\mathcal{E}_{n,1}
: For all
0\leq t\leq n-1
,
F(\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{t}))<F(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})-\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})
.

2.

The event
\mathcal{E}_{n,2}
: There exists
\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\in(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq n-1}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})\cap\mathcal{S}\neq\{\}
, and for all
\boldsymbol{w}_{s}
with
0\leq s<t
, we have
F(\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{s}))<F(\boldsymbol{w}_{s})-\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{s})
.

That is,
\mathcal{E}_{n}=\mathcal{E}_{n,1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{n,2}
. Then over the randomness in
\mathcal{A}
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\geq p_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
.

Proof.
We proceed by induction on
n
. The base case
n=0
is vacuously evident, and the case
n=1
follows immediately by the definition of a decrease procedure from
Definition
2.2
and hypotheses of
Theorem
2.1
.

For the inductive step, suppose
Lemma
B.1
is true for some
n\geq 1
; we show it is for
n+1
. By the inductive hypothesis, we know that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\geq p_{n}
. We aim to show
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\geq p_{n+1}
. If
p_{n}\leq 0
there is nothing to prove, so suppose now that
n
is such that
p_{n}>0
.

1.

Let
p=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{n,2}|\mathcal{E}_{n}\right)
. Note
\mathcal{E}_{n,2}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{n+1,2}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{n+1}
.

2.

Let
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{E}_{n,1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{n,2}^{c}
. Thus, if
\mathcal{B}
occurs, then all the
(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq n-1}
are such that
F(\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{t}))<F(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})-\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})
, but
\mathcal{E}_{n,2}
did not occur. Note
\mathcal{E}_{n}
is the disjoint union
\mathcal{E}_{n,2}\sqcup\mathcal{B}
, so
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}|\mathcal{E}_{n})=1-p
.

Under
\mathcal{B}
, we know
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{w}_{n-1})
is such that
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{n})\leq F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
. Hence
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
. Therefore, conditioned on
\mathcal{B}
, by the hypotheses of
Theorem
2.1
we have with probability at least
p_{0}
that either
F(\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{n}))<F(\boldsymbol{w}_{n})-\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{n})
or
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{n})\cap\mathcal{S}\neq\{\}
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the event that
F(\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{n}))<F(\boldsymbol{w}_{n})-\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{n})
occurs. Let
\mathcal{D}
be the event that
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{n})\cap\mathcal{S}\neq\{\}
occurs but
\mathcal{C}
does not occur.
Recall that
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
conditioned on
\mathcal{B}
. Furthermore recall that
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{w}_{n})
is only a function of
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}
, and none of the
(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq n-1}
.
Thus the definition of decrease procedure,
Definition
2.2
, implies that

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}\sqcup\mathcal{D}|\mathcal{B}\right)\geq p_{0}.

Now since
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B})=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}|\mathcal{E}_{n})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\geq(1-p)p_{n}>0
, Bayes’ Rule implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{C})\sqcup(\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{D})|\mathcal{B}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}\cap((\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{C})\sqcup(\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{D})))}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}\cap(\mathcal{C}\sqcup\mathcal{D}))}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B})}=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}\sqcup\mathcal{D}|\mathcal{B}\right)\geq p_{0}.

Note
\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{C}
implies that
\mathcal{E}_{n+1,1}
occurs, since under
\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{C}
we have
F(\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{t}))<F(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})-\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})
for all
0\leq t\leq n
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{D}
implies that
\mathcal{E}_{n+1,2}
occurs, since under
\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{D}
we have
F(\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{t}))<F(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})-\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})
for
0\leq t\leq n-1
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{n})\cap\mathcal{S}\neq\{\}
.

Thus recalling
\mathcal{E}_{n,2},\mathcal{B}
are disjoint, we see that
\mathcal{E}_{n+1}
contains the following disjoint union of events:

\mathcal{E}_{n+1}\supseteq\mathcal{E}_{n,2}\sqcup(\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{C})\sqcup(\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{D}).

The above observations imply via Bayes’ Rule that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{n+1})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{n,2}\sqcup(\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{C})\sqcup(\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{D})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{n,2}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{C})\sqcup(\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{D})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{n,2}|\mathcal{E}_{n}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{n}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{C})\sqcup(\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{D})|\mathcal{B}\right)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}|\mathcal{E}_{n})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\left(p+\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{C})\sqcup(\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{D})|\mathcal{B}\right)\cdot(1-p)\right)

\displaystyle\geq p_{n}\left(p+p_{0}(1-p)\right)

\displaystyle\geq p_{n}(p_{0}p+p_{0}(1-p))=p_{n}p_{0}\geq p_{n+1}.

Here we used that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\geq p_{n}
,
p_{n}p_{0}\geq p_{n+1}
which follows immediately from the definition of
p_{n}
,
p_{0}\leq 1
, and simple manipulations. The inductive step, and hence the proof, is thus complete.
∎

Using
Lemma
B.1
now readily proves the following:

Claim 3
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be the event that there exists
\boldsymbol{w}_{t}
with
\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\in(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T-1}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})\cap\mathcal{S}\neq\{\}
, and for all
\boldsymbol{w}_{s}
with
0\leq s<t
, we have
F(\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{s}))<F(\boldsymbol{w}_{s})-\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{s})
. Then
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\geq p_{T}
.

Proof of
Claim
3
.

Apply
Lemma
B.1
with
n=T
. Following the notation from there, we have that the event
\mathcal{E}_{T}=\mathcal{E}_{T,1}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T,2}
has probability at least
p_{T}
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{E}_{T,1}
occurs. Note
\mathcal{E}_{T,1}
implies that
\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
for all
0\leq t\leq T
. Therefore

\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})\geq\inf_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}}\Delta(\boldsymbol{u})\text{ for all }0\leq t\leq T.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:frameworkpflowerbounddecrease}

Moreover, telescoping the direct implication of
\mathcal{E}_{T,1}
gives that

F(\boldsymbol{w}_{T})<F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})-\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{t}).\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:frameworkpfdecreasetelescope}

Combining
(
B
)
and
(
B
)
and recalling that we shifted WLOG so
F
has minimum value 0 (see Notation) gives

T\inf_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}}\Delta(\boldsymbol{u})\leq\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})<F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})-F(\boldsymbol{w}_{T})\leq F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

This contradicts our choice of
T
.

Thus
\mathcal{E}_{T,1}
cannot occur, and so
\mathcal{E}_{T,2}
must occur, i.e.
\mathcal{E}_{T}=\mathcal{E}_{T,2}
. Note
\mathcal{E}_{T,2}
is exactly the event
\mathcal{E}
. Thus

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{T,2})=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{T})\geq p_{T},

as desired.
∎

Conditioning on the event
\mathcal{E}
from
Claim
3
, by
Claim
3
, we immediately recover the desired guarantee on the output, probability, and number of candidate vectors stated in
Theorem
2.1
. The only part remaining to prove
Theorem
2.1
is to establish the bound
N=\frac{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\overline{\Delta}}+\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}}t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{u})
on the number of oracle calls.

To this end, condition on
\mathcal{E}
from
Claim
3
in all of the following, and follow the notation from there, in particular the definition of
\boldsymbol{w}_{t}
. Directly, we obtain that the number of oracle calls is at most
\sum_{i=0}^{t}t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})
(the last term
t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})
in the sum appears since computing
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})
and
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})
takes at most
t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})
oracle calls). We now upper bound this sum.

As we are conditioning on
\mathcal{E}
and since we assumed WLOG by shifting that
F
has minimum value 0, we have

F(\boldsymbol{w}_{i+1})-F(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})<-\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})<0\text{ for all }0\leq i\leq t-1\implies\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})<F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})-F(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})\leq F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:frameworkpfdecreasesumfinish}

The above also implies
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})\leq F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, for all
0\leq i\leq t
. Therefore,
t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}}t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{u})
for all
0\leq i\leq t
. Thus
(
B
)
gives

\frac{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})}>\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})}{\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})}\geq\min_{0\leq i\leq t-1}\frac{\Delta(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})}{t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})}\geq\overline{\Delta},

where the last inequality uses the elementary inequality
\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}b_{i}}\geq\min_{i}\frac{a_{i}}{b_{i}}
for
a_{i}\geq 0,b_{i}>0
, that
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
for all
0\leq i\leq t-1
, and the definition of
\overline{\Delta}
.
Rearranging and recalling
t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}}t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{u})
as justified above, we obtain

\sum_{i=0}^{t}t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}}t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{u})+\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i})\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}}t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{u})+\frac{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\overline{\Delta}}.

This yields the desired conclusion on oracle complexity, completing the proof.
∎

Appendix C
First Order Convergence Proofs

C.1
Proofs for Adaptive GD

Proof.
As with the proof of
Theorem
3.1
, we use
Theorem
2.1
. We again have
\mathcal{S}=\{\boldsymbol{w}:\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq\varepsilon\}
, and recall the choice of
\eta
from
Theorem
3.2
. Now we let
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\eta_{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}),\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\right)
. Thus
\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\eta\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
, and
t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=1
.

Claim 4
.

For any
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
in the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set
\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
,
\mathcal{A}
is a
(\mathcal{S},1,\min\left\{\frac{L_{1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{2\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)^{2}},\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\right\},0,\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
-decrease procedure.

To show this, analogously to the proof of
Theorem
3.1
, for any
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\not\in\mathcal{S}
in the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set
\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, we will show that the function will deterministically decrease by strictly greater than
\min\left\{\frac{L_{1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)^{2}},\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\right\}
at the next iterate. By definition of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, exactly as with the proof of
Theorem
3.1
, we conclude via
Theorem
2.1
upon showing
Claim
4
.

To show
Claim
4
, by choice of step size, we have
\eta_{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}
. Thus

\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\right\|\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})+1)}.

Now combining
Lemma
3.1
with
Assumption
1.1
, and because
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, we see for all
\boldsymbol{p}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}}
,
\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{p})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
where
L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
is defined as in the statement of
Theorem
3.2
. We thus obtain by
Lemma
A.1
,

F(\boldsymbol{u}_{1})\leq F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}+\frac{L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta^{2}}{2}\cdot\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:adaptivegddescent}

Recall that
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\not\in\mathcal{S}
, so
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|>\varepsilon
. We break into cases:

1.

If
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|>\frac{L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}
, then
\eta_{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}=\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|}
. In this case, substituting into
(
C.1
)
gives

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{u}_{1})

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}+\frac{L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta^{2}}{2}\cdot\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|+\frac{L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{2\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle<F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)^{2}}.

2.

Else if
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|\leq L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
, then
\eta_{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}=\frac{1}{L_{1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
. In this case, substituting into
(
C.1
)
gives

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{u}_{1})

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}+\frac{L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta^{2}}{2}\cdot\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\frac{\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}}{2L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}<F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})},

where we used that
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|>\varepsilon
.

In either case, for
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|>\varepsilon
we have that

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{u}_{1})

\displaystyle<F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\min\left\{\frac{L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{2\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)^{2}},\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2L^{\prime}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\right\}.

This proves
Claim
4
. By our framework
Theorem
2.1
, the proof is complete.
∎

C.2
Proofs for SGD for FOSPs

Here, we prove
Theorem
3.3
. We first introduce technical preliminaries, which will also be used in
Section
E
.

Theorem C.1

(Vector-Valued Azuma-Hoeffding, Theorem 3.5 in
Pinelis (1994)
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be such that for all
k
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{k}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]=0
,
\left\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{k}\right\|^{2}\leq\sigma_{k}^{2}
. Then for any
\lambda>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{k}\right\|\geq\lambda\right)\leq 4\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sigma_{k}^{2}}\right).

Note the bound here is dimension free, so this result does not follow directly from standard Azuma-Hoeffding. Such a result can also be found in
Kallenberg and Sztencel (1991)
;
Zhang (2005)
;
Fang et al. (2019)
.

Theorem C.2

(Data-Dependent Concentration Inequality, Lemma 3 in
Rakhlin et al. (2012)
)
.

Let
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{K}\in\mathbb{R}
be such that for all
k
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\varepsilon_{k}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]=0
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]\leq\sigma_{k}^{2}
. Furthermore suppose that
\mathbb{P}\left(\varepsilon_{k}\leq b|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right)=1
. Letting
V_{K}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sigma_{k}^{2}
, for any
\delta<1/e
,
K\geq 4
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\varepsilon_{k}>2\max\left\{2\sqrt{V_{k}},b\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\right\}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\right)\leq\delta\log(K).

Such a result is also presented in
Zhang (2005)
;
Bartlett et al. (2008)
;
Fang et al. (2019)
.

We will first prove
Theorem
3.3
in the case where
\left\|\nabla f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\zeta})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|
is bounded by
\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{w}))
. As noted in
Fang et al. (2019)
, these same inequalities hold when the martingale difference is not bounded or almost-surely bounded but rather the norms are sub-Gaussian with parameter
\sigma_{k}
. Thus after the proof, we remark how to straightforwardly generalize
Theorem
3.3
to the case when
\left\|\nabla f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\zeta})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|
is sub-Gaussian with parameter
\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{w}))
in
Remark
7
.

Now, we prove
Theorem
3.3
.

Proof.
We use our framework
Theorem
2.1
with
\mathcal{S}=\{\boldsymbol{w}:\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq\varepsilon\}
. Recall as per the discussion of SGD in our framework in
Subsection
2.3
, we let
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
, and define a sequence
(\boldsymbol{p}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq K_{0}}
via

\boldsymbol{p}_{i}=\boldsymbol{p}_{i-1}-\eta\nabla f(\boldsymbol{p}_{i-1};\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{i}),

where the
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{i}
are minibatch samples i.i.d. across different
i
. Note this sequence can be equivalently defined by repeated compositions of the function
\boldsymbol{u}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}-\eta\nabla f(\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\zeta})
.

We now let
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\left(\boldsymbol{p}_{K_{0}},(\boldsymbol{p}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq K_{0}-1}\right)
, hence
\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\boldsymbol{p}_{K_{0}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=(\boldsymbol{p}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq K_{0}-1}
. Thus
t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=K_{0}
. Also note the noise
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}
used defining
\mathcal{A}
are independent across different
t
.

For appropriate
\eta=\tilde{\Theta}(\varepsilon^{2})
,
K_{0}=\tilde{\Theta}(\varepsilon^{-2})
depending only on
\varepsilon,\delta,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
and polylogarithmically in
1/\delta
, which we define below, we establish the following
Claim
5
:

Claim 5
.

For any
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
in the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set
\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
,
\mathcal{A}
is a
(\mathcal{S},K_{0},\frac{\eta K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}{4},p,\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
-decrease procedure, where
p=\frac{\delta\eta K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}{4(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}
.

Then using
Theorem
2.1
, we then directly conclude
Theorem
3.3
.

To show
Claim
5
, consider any
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
in the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set but not in
\mathcal{S}
.
Following the notation from above, consider a ‘block’ of
K_{0}
consecutive iterates of SGD starting at
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
. We establish that with probability at least
1-p
, if none of the iterates
\{\boldsymbol{p}_{0}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\ldots,\boldsymbol{p}_{K_{0}-1}\}
lie in
\mathcal{S}
, then
F(\boldsymbol{p}_{K_{0}})<F(\boldsymbol{p}_{0})-\Delta
where
\Delta=\frac{\eta K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}{4}
. Then recalling the definitions of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, we immediately conclude
Claim
5
.

Definitions and Parameters:

For convenience, define

\displaystyle L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1),

\displaystyle L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1),

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1),

\displaystyle B(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Also define

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}=\nabla f(\boldsymbol{p}_{t};\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t+1})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),

where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t+1}
denotes the i.i.d. minibatch samples. Note by
Assumption
3.1
that
\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right]=0
, where expectation is with respect to
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t+1}
.

In particular, we choose these parameters as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\varepsilon)^{6}\log(1/\delta)^{6}}

\displaystyle K_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log(1/\tilde{\eta})^{2}\log(1/\delta)^{2}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2},

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\max\left\{1,\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\right\}}\cdot\tilde{\eta},

where

\displaystyle C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=128B(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\lor 64(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)^{2},

\displaystyle\tilde{L}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=8L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})(L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}+\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2})\lor 2L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\lor 4\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=\tilde{L}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\lor(3\sqrt{2}\log(\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})))^{8}\lor(3\sqrt{2})^{8}.

Remark 6
.

Note that
C,\tilde{L}^{\prime},\tilde{L}
depend only polynomially in terms of the self-bounding functions
\rho_{0},\rho_{1}
,
\sigma
, and
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
.

Note we can assume WLOG that
\varepsilon
and the desired probability
\delta
are at most some small enough
universal
constants in
(0,1)
; by doing so, the result does not change up to universal constant, and hence is identical under the
O(\cdot)
. Consequently we may assume WLOG that
\tilde{\eta}
and
\eta
are at most some small enough universal constant in
(0,1)
and that
K_{0}\geq 4
.

Claim 6
.

For
\varepsilon,\delta
small enough universal constants, the above choice of parameters satisfies the following properties:

\displaystyle\max\left\{1,\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\right\}\eta

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}\leq\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{8L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})(L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}+\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2})},\frac{1}{2K_{0}L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})},\frac{1}{4\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sqrt{K_{0}\log(4K_{0}/p)}}\right\},

(12)

\displaystyle K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle\geq 128B(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log\left(\frac{2\log K_{0}}{p}\right).

(13)

For the sake of brevity, we prove
Claim
6
after the our main proof. Checking this is a matter of elementary, albeit tedious, univariate inequalities.

Again, our plan is to apply
Theorem
2.1
by showing decrease with high probability for a block of
K_{0}
iterates starting at
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}
.

Notation:

Let
\mathfrak{F}^{t}
denote the filtration of all information up through
\boldsymbol{p}_{t}
, but
not
including the minibatch sample
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t+1}
. Let
\mathcal{K}
be a stopping time denoting the first
t
such that
\boldsymbol{p}_{t}\not\in\mathbb{B}\left(\boldsymbol{p}_{0},\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}\right)
, i.e. the escape time of the iterates beginning at
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}
from
\mathbb{B}\left(\boldsymbol{p}_{0},\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}\right)=\mathbb{B}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}\right)
.

We first detail two high probability events we will condition on for the remainder of the proof:

•

By Vector-Valued Azuma Hoeffding
Theorem
C.1
, for a given
1\leq t\leq K_{0}
we have with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{2K_{0}}
,

\left\|\eta\sum_{k=1}^{t}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}\right\|\leq 2\eta\sqrt{\log(48K_{0}/p)\sum_{k=1}^{t}\sigma\left(F(\boldsymbol{p}_{k-1})\right)^{2}}=2\eta\sqrt{\log(4K_{0}/p)\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\sigma\left(F(\boldsymbol{p}_{k-1})\right)^{2}}.

This follows since each
\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]=0
as the stochastic gradient oracle is unbiased, and as
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}\right\|\leq\sigma\left(F(\boldsymbol{p}_{k-1})\right)
by
Assumption
3.1
.

Thus by Union Bound, with probability at least
1-p/2
, we have for all
1\leq t\leq K_{0}
that

\left\|\eta\sum_{k=1}^{t}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}\right\|\leq 2\eta\sqrt{\log(4K_{0}/p)\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\sigma\left(F(\boldsymbol{p}_{k})\right)^{2}}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:noiseupperboundsgdfirstorder}

Denote this event by
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, so
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\geq 1-p/2
.

•

We define a stochastic process with the following trick to derive uniform bounds. Define the following sequence of real numbers:

Y_{t}:=-\eta\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\rangle 1_{t<\mathcal{K}}.

Notice
1_{t<\mathcal{K}}
is
\mathfrak{F}^{t}
-measurable, as
\{t<\mathcal{K}\}
holds if and only if
\boldsymbol{p}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{p}_{t}\in\mathbb{B}\left(\boldsymbol{p}_{0},\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}\right)
.

Clearly
\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})
is also
\mathfrak{F}^{t}
-measurable. Thus as the stochastic gradient oracle is unbiased (i.e.
\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}|\mathfrak{F}^{t}\right]=0
),

\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\rangle 1_{t<\mathcal{K}}|\mathfrak{F}^{t}\right]=0.

For
t\geq\mathcal{K}
we have
Y_{t}\equiv 0
. For
t<\mathcal{K}
, we have
\boldsymbol{p}_{t}\in\mathbb{B}\left(\boldsymbol{p}_{0},\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}\right)
. Consequently by
Lemma
3.1
and
Corollary
1
we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert Y_{t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\eta\left\lvert\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\|\leq\eta\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\|.

Moreover by
Assumption
3.1
and
Lemma
3.1
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\|\leq\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}))\leq\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)=\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

In particular, recall that
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}
is the difference between the gradient oracle and actual gradient at
\boldsymbol{p}_{t}
.

By the above arguments, both of the following inequalities hold deterministically:

\displaystyle\left\lvert Y_{t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),

\displaystyle\left\lvert Y_{t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\eta\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})=\eta L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

We now apply both of these bounds in Data-Dependent Concentration Inequality,
Theorem
C.2
(whose conditions hold because we can assume
\delta,\varepsilon
are at most given universal constants, so
K_{0}\geq 4,2\log K_{0}/p>e
). Consequently we obtain with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{2}
that

\displaystyle-\eta\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\rangle 1_{t<\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log\left(\frac{2\log K_{0}}{p}\right)\bigvee

\displaystyle 4\sqrt{\eta^{2}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|^{2}}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{2\log K_{0}}{p}\right)}.

(15)

Denote this event by
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, so
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2})\geq 1-p/2
.

For the rest of this proof, we condition on
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
. By the above,
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}
occurs with probability at least
1-p
. Denote
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
.

A-priori, these bounds are not particularly useful, especially in our more challenging setting under
Assumption
3.2
where noise can depend on function value. However conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
, we prove that SGD is sufficiently ‘local’, in particular that
\left\|\boldsymbol{p}_{t}-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\right\|\leq 1
for all
t,1\leq t\leq K_{0}
. This will then give us control over function value via
Lemma
3.1
, which then allow us to make use of these bounds in a more standard way.

Lemma C.1
.

Conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
(and hence conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
), for all
t,1\leq t\leq K_{0}
, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{p}_{t}-\boldsymbol{p}_{0}\right\|=\left\|\boldsymbol{p}_{t}-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\right\|\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}.

Proof.
We go by induction on
t
. Notice after
t
iterates,

\boldsymbol{p}_{t}=\boldsymbol{w}_{0}-\eta\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{k})-\eta\sum_{k=1}^{t}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}.

For the base case
t=1
, we have from
Corollary
1
that
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\right\|\leq\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))\leq L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
. From the definition of the high-probability event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and properties of
\eta
from
Claim
6
, and as
\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\geq\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
), it follows that

\left\|\eta\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}\right\|\leq 2\eta\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))\sqrt{K_{0}\log\left(4K_{0}/p\right)}\leq\frac{1}{2\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}.

Consequently by properties of
\eta
from
Claim
6
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{p}_{1}-\boldsymbol{p}_{0}\right\|\leq\left\|\eta\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\right\|+\left\|\eta\boldsymbol{\xi}_{0}\right\|\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}.

This finishes the proof of the base case.

Now suppose
Lemma
C.1
holds for all
1\leq k\leq t-1
; we will show it for
t
. From
Lemma
3.1
, for all
k\leq t-1
, we have

\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{k})\right\|\leq\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\leq L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Thus for each
k
, we have

\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{p}_{k}))\leq\sigma\left(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1\right)=\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Thus conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{p}_{t}-\boldsymbol{p}_{0}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\eta\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{k})\right\|+\left\|\eta\sum_{k=1}^{t}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\eta K_{0}L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+2\eta\sqrt{\log\left(4K_{0}/p\right)\sum_{k=0}^{K_{0}-1}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\eta K_{0}L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+2\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sqrt{K_{0}\log\left(4K_{0}/p\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}+\frac{1}{2\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}=\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}.

Here we used the choice of
\eta
from
Claim
6
and the upper bound
(
•
‣
C.2
)
on
\left\|\eta\sum_{k=1}^{t}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}\right\|
implied by
\mathcal{E}_{1}
. This completes the induction.
∎

Now that we know the iterates of SGD are ‘sufficiently local’ for
K_{0}
iterations via
Lemma
C.1
, the finish is straightforward. Condition on
\mathcal{E}
for the rest of the proof. Consider any
0\leq t\leq K_{0}-1
.
\mathcal{E}
implies for all
\boldsymbol{p}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{p}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{p}_{t}}
, writing
\boldsymbol{p}=\theta\boldsymbol{p}_{t-1}+(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{p}_{t}
for
\theta\in[0,1]
, that we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{p}_{0}\right\|\leq\theta\left\|\boldsymbol{p}_{t-1}-\boldsymbol{p}_{0}\right\|+(1-\theta)\left\|\boldsymbol{p}_{t}-\boldsymbol{p}_{0}\right\|\leq\left(1-\theta+\theta\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}=\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}.

Consequently
F(\boldsymbol{p})\leq\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)
, so the above combined with
Assumption
1.1
gives

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{p})\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:sgdfirstorderhessianbound}

We also obtain from
Lemma
C.1
together with
Corollary
1
and
Assumption
3.1
that for all
0\leq t\leq K_{0}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sigma\left(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1\right)=\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\rho_{0}\left(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1\right)=L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

(17)

Now by
Lemma
A.1
and
(
C.2
)
,

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})-\eta\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\nabla f(\boldsymbol{p}_{t};\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t+1})\right\rangle+\frac{\eta^{2}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{2}\left\|\nabla f(\boldsymbol{p}_{t};\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})-\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|^{2}-\eta\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1})\right\rangle+\eta^{2}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left(\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\|^{2}\right).

The last step uses the definition of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}
and Young’s Inequality.

Summing and telescoping the above for
0\leq t\leq K_{0}-1
, and applying
(
17
)
, gives

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{p}_{K_{0}})

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{p}_{0})-\eta\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|^{2}-\eta\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\eta^{2}K_{0}L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+\eta^{2}K_{0}\sigma^{2}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

(18)

Now, conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
, we upper bound

-\eta\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\rangle

using
(
15
)
. Under
\mathcal{E}
, by
Lemma
C.1
and
Lemma
3.1
, we have
\boldsymbol{p}_{t}\in\mathbb{B}\left(\boldsymbol{p}_{0},\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}\right)
for all
1\leq t\leq K_{0}
, which implies that
t<\mathcal{K}
for all
1\leq t\leq K_{0}
. Therefore

-\eta\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\rangle=-\eta\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\rangle 1_{t<\mathcal{K}}.

Now AM-GM gives

\displaystyle 4\sqrt{\eta^{2}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|^{2}}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{2\log K_{0}}{p}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta\left(\frac{1}{4}\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|^{2}+8\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\log\left(\frac{2\log K_{0}}{p}\right)\right).

Combining with
(
15
)
, we obtain

\displaystyle-\eta\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\eta\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}\right\rangle 1_{t<\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta}{2}\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|^{2}+16\eta B(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log\left(\frac{2\log K_{0}}{p}\right).

Combining with
(
18
)
gives

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{p}_{K_{0}})

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{p}_{0})-\frac{\eta}{2}\sum_{t=0}^{K_{0}-1}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|^{2}+16\eta B(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log\left(\frac{2\log K_{0}}{p}\right)+\eta^{2}K_{0}L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\eta^{2}K_{0}\sigma^{2}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

(19)

Suppose that
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\right\|>\varepsilon
for all
0\leq t\leq K_{0}-1
. Then the above gives

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{p}_{K_{0}})

\displaystyle<F(\boldsymbol{p}_{0})-\frac{\eta K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}{2}+16\eta B(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log\left(\frac{2\log K_{0}}{p}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\eta^{2}K_{0}L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+\eta^{2}K_{0}\sigma^{2}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

To make use of this bound, by our choice of
\eta
,
Claim
6
implies that

\eta^{2}K_{0}L_{0}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+\eta^{2}K_{0}\sigma^{2}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\leq\frac{\eta K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}{8}.

By choice of
K_{0}
,
Claim
6
implies that

16\eta B(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log\left(\frac{2\log K_{0}}{p}\right)\leq\frac{\eta K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}{8}.

The above was all conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
, which occurred with probability at least
1-p
. Thus by
(
19
)
, we obtain that with this same probability which is at least
1-p
, if none of
\boldsymbol{p}_{0},\ldots,\boldsymbol{p}_{K_{0}-1}
have gradient norm larger than
\varepsilon
, we have

F(\boldsymbol{p}_{K_{0}})<F(\boldsymbol{p}_{0})-\frac{\eta K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}{4}=F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\frac{\eta K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}{4}.

This establishes that
\mathcal{A}
is a
(\mathcal{S},K_{0}+1,\frac{\eta K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}{4},p,\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
-decrease procedure. Following our initial observations, we conclude via
Theorem
2.1
.
∎

Now we prove
Claim
6
.

Proof of
Claim
6
.

We first prove
(
13
)
. Recall we chose

K_{0}=\frac{C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log(1/\tilde{\eta})^{2}\log(1/\delta)^{2}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}.

Furthermore recall
p=\frac{\delta\tilde{\eta}K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}{4(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}
. Thus,
(
13
)
holds if and only if

C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\tilde{\eta})^{2}\log(1/\delta)^{2}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}\geq 128B(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log\left(\frac{8\log K_{0}\cdot(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}{\delta\tilde{\eta}K_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

As
C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\geq 128B(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\lor 64(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)^{2}
, again using the expression for
K_{0}
, it suffices to prove

\log(1/\tilde{\eta})^{2}\log(1/\delta)^{2}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}\geq\log\left(\frac{\log K_{0}}{C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/2}\delta\tilde{\eta}\log(1/\tilde{\eta})^{2}\log(1/\delta)^{2}}\right).

As
\log(1/\delta)
,
\log(1/\tilde{\eta})
are both larger than 1, it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\log(1/\tilde{\eta})^{2}\log(1/\delta)^{2}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\log(1/\tilde{\eta})+\log(1/\delta)

\displaystyle+\log\left(\frac{\log C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+\log(1/\varepsilon^{2})+2\log\log(1/\tilde{\eta})+2\log\log(1/\delta)+2\log\log(1/\varepsilon)}{C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/2}}\right).

Since
C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\geq 64
, it satisfies
\log C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})<C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/2}
, so it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\log(1/\tilde{\eta})^{2}\log(1/\delta)^{2}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\log(1/\tilde{\eta})+\log(1/\delta)

\displaystyle+\log\left(1+2\log(1/\varepsilon)+2\log\log(1/\tilde{\eta})+2\log\log(1/\delta)+2\log\log(1/\varepsilon)\right).

By comparing ‘degrees’, we conclude recalling we can assume WLOG that
\delta,\varepsilon,\tilde{\eta}
are smaller than some universal constant.

Now we prove
(
12
)
. We will prove that

\tilde{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{\tilde{L}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})K_{0}\sqrt{\log(4K_{0}/p)}}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:newetaparamchocesgdfirstorder}

After proving
(
C.2
)
, recalling our choice of
K_{0}>1/\varepsilon^{2}
directly implies
(
12
)
. To show
(
C.2
)
, equivalently, we want to show

\tilde{\eta}\log(1/\tilde{\eta})^{2}\sqrt{\log(4K_{0}/p)}\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\tilde{L}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\delta)^{2}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}}.

Recalling the definition of
p
, this holds if and only if

\tilde{\eta}\log(1/\tilde{\eta})^{2}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{16(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}{\delta\tilde{\eta}\varepsilon^{2}}\right)}\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\tilde{L}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\delta)^{2}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}}.

Now we explicitly recall our expression for
\tilde{\eta}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\varepsilon)^{6}\log(1/\delta)^{6}}
. Plugging this in and recalling
\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\geq\tilde{L}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}C(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}
, it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/2}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{6}\log(1/\delta)^{6}}\log\left(\frac{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\varepsilon)^{6}\log(1/\delta)^{6}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\cdot\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{16(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\varepsilon)^{6}\log(1/\delta)^{6}}{\delta\varepsilon^{4}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\log(1/\delta)^{2}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}}.

Thus it suffices to prove:

\displaystyle\frac{18}{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/2}}\log\left(\frac{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{16(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\delta)}{\delta\varepsilon}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\log(1/\delta)^{4}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{4}.

Recall
\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/8}\geq 3\sqrt{2}\log(\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))\lor 3\sqrt{2}
and so

\displaystyle\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/4}}\log\left(\frac{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/4}}\left(\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\log(1/\delta)+\log\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/8}}\left(1+\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\log(1/\delta)\right).

Thus it suffices to show

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/4}}\left(1+\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\log(1/\delta)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\cdot\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{16(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\delta)}{\delta\varepsilon}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\log(1/\delta)^{4}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{4}.

To this end recall
\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/8}\geq\log(16(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))
, thus

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/8}}\log\left(\frac{16(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\log(1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\delta)}{\delta\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{1/8}}\left(\log(16(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\tilde{L}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))+\log(1/\delta)+\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\log(1/\delta))+\log\log(1/\varepsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\log(1/\delta)+\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\log(1/\delta))+\log\log(1/\varepsilon).

Therefore it suffices to show

\displaystyle\left(1+\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\log(1/\delta)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(1+\log(1/\delta)+\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\log(1/\delta))+\log\log(1/\varepsilon)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\log(1/\delta)^{4}\log(1/\varepsilon)^{4}.

Evidently the above holds for small enough universal constants
\delta,\varepsilon
(compare ‘degrees’), so we conclude the proof.
∎

Remark 7
.

We also discuss how to extend this result to when the
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\right\|
has sub-Gaussianity parameter
\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}))
. The extension is straightforward. Again, we aim to prove
Claim
5
. For the rest of this remark, follow the notation from the proof for SGD above. Besides applying
Theorem
C.1
,
Theorem
C.2
when the relevant random variables are sub-Gaussian, which still hold true as mentioned in
Fang et al. (2019)
, the only other time we used that
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\right\|\leq\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}))
holds deterministically is to derive
(
18
)
.

We apply
Theorem
C.1
,
Theorem
C.2
identically to the proof earlier. This time, we have for
t<\mathcal{K}
that
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t+1}
is sub-Gaussian with parameter
\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
, thanks to the same trick of multiplying with
1_{t<\mathcal{K}}
when applying
Theorem
C.2
.

The only change is as follows: in the definition
\mathcal{E}
, add in the intersection the event
\mathcal{E}_{3}
that for all
1\leq t\leq K_{0}
,
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\right\|^{2}\leq\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}))^{2}\log(K_{0}/p)
, where
p
is defined the same as before. We control the probability of
\mathcal{E}_{3}
via the following Lemma:

Lemma C.2

(Equivalent of Lemma 12,
De Sa et al. (2022)
)
.

With probability at least
1-p
, we have for all
1\leq t\leq K_{0}
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\right\|^{2}\leq\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}))^{2}\log(K_{0}/p).

Proof.
By
Assumption
3.1
, with probability
1-\frac{p}{K_{0}}
, we have

\frac{\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\right\|^{2}}{\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t}))^{2}}\leq\log(K_{0}/p).

A Union Bound finishes the proof.
∎

Now we condition on
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{E}_{3}
, which has probability at least
1-2p
by combining our earlier argument with
Lemma
C.2
. Note this only changes the resulting guarantee by a universal constant. We still have
Lemma
C.1
, which does not require an upper bound on
each

\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\right\|
in its proof but simply uses concentration from event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
.

Thus, conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
, we still have
F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t})\leq F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1
by
Lemma
C.1
,
Lemma
3.1
, and as
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
. Now conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
, by
Lemma
C.2
, we still have the following upper bound for all
1\leq t\leq K_{0}
:

\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\right\|^{2}\leq\sigma(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)^{2}\log(K_{0}/p)=\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\log(K_{0}/p).

Therefore conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
, we can still derive a bound analogous to
(
18
)
. This resulting bound changes by only a
\log(K_{0}/p)
factor (from
Lemma
C.2
, see the above display); moreover recall
K_{0},p
depend polynomially in
\delta,1/\varepsilon
. By adjusting
\eta
smaller by a
\polylog(K_{0}/p)
factor, the same proof as above goes through, up to changing quantities by polylogarithmic factors.

Appendix D
Perturbed GD finding Second Order Stationary Points

D.1
Proof using the Framework

Here we prove
Theorem
3.4
. We instantiate
Algorithm
1
formally here. The parameters of
Algorithm
1
will depend on
L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
, which are defined in
(
3.2
)
,
(
21
)
respectively, and depend only on
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
. Given a desired success probability
1-\delta
for
\delta>0
, a tolerance
\varepsilon>0
, and
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})

1.

c\leq c_{\text{max}}
is a universal constant, where

.

2.

\tilde{\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon}{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
.

\chi\leftarrow 4\max\left\{\log\left(\frac{2dL_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{c^{2}\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2.5}\delta}\right),5\right\}
.

4.

.

.

Algorithm 1

Perturbed Gradient Descent, modified from
Jin et al. (2017)
.

\tilde{\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon}{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
,
\chi\leftarrow 4\max\left\{\log\left(\frac{2dL_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{c\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2.5}\delta}\right),5\right\}
,
\eta\leftarrow\frac{c}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
,
r\leftarrow\frac{\sqrt{c}\tilde{\varepsilon}}{\chi^{2}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
,

,

,
t_{\text{thres}}\leftarrow\frac{\chi}{c^{2}}\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}}
. Here

in

.

while
True

if

\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})\right\|\leq g_{\text{thres}}

then

\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{t}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{w}_{t}
,

,
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}
uniform from
\mathbb{B}(\vec{\boldsymbol{0}},r)

while

do

\boldsymbol{w}_{t+1}=\boldsymbol{w}_{t}-\eta\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}_{t})
,

,

\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{t_{\text{noise}}}

,

Proof of
Theorem
3.4
given
Lemma
D.2
.

We will first prove the following Lemma, which will define
L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
and explain its significance.

Lemma D.1
.

Define
L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
as in
(
3.2
)
, and define

\displaystyle L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=\max\left\{1,L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),\rho_{2}\left(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1\right)\right\}.

(21)

Then we have the following:

1.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{u}
is such that
\left\|\boldsymbol{u}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}
, where
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set. Then under
Assumption
1.1
(and in particular under
Assumption
1.2
),

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{u})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

2.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{2}
are such that
\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|,\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{2}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}
, where
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
. Then

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{u}_{1})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{u}_{2})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\right\|.

Remark 8
.

Note
L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\geq 1
, and
L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\geq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
.

Proof of
Lemma
D.1
.

Recall by
Corollary
1
that
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}))
. Now by
Lemma
3.1
and as
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, for any
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}
with
\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})+1)}
, we have
F(\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime})\leq F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})+1
.
The first part now directly follows by
Assumption
1.1
.

The second part now follows by noting the line segment
\overline{\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}}
is contained in
\mathbb{B}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}},\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}\right)
via Triangle Inequality, recalling
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, and then applying
Lemma
A.6
and
Lemma
3.1
.
∎

We now prove
Theorem
3.4
by instantiating our framework.

Define
\tilde{\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon}{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
as we did earlier, and note
L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\geq 1
. It suffices to show for
\tilde{\varepsilon}\leq 1
, that with probability at least
1-\delta
, we will return
\boldsymbol{w}
such that
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq\tilde{\varepsilon}
,
\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\succeq-\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{I}
in
T=O\left(\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\max\left\{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),1\right\}\chi^{4}}{\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\max\left\{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),1\right\}\chi^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
oracle calls.
6
6

6

The
\max\left\{1,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\right\}
is a proof artifact.

Now let the set of interest

\mathcal{S}=\{\boldsymbol{w}:\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq g_{\text{thres}},\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\succeq-\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{I}\}.

Note
g_{\text{thres}}\leq\tilde{\varepsilon}
, so
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathcal{S}
immediately implies
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq\tilde{\varepsilon}
,
\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\succeq-\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{I}
. Also note it suffices to show the result for all
\tilde{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{100L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
; otherwise for larger
\tilde{\varepsilon}
we can just apply the result for
\tilde{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{100L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
. Thus as
L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\geq 1
, we can assume
\tilde{\varepsilon}\leq 1
. Clearly, we also can assume WLOG that
t_{\text{thres}}\geq 1
.

As in
Subsection
2.3
, we make the following definitions for
Algorithm
1
. For all
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|>g_{\text{thres}}
, we let

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\eta\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}),\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\right),\text{ hence }\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\eta\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}),\mathcal{A}_{2}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}.

Otherwise if
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|\leq g_{\text{thres}}
, we let
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\xi}
where
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is uniform from
\mathbb{B}(\vec{\boldsymbol{0}},r)
, and define a sequence
(\boldsymbol{p}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq t_{\text{thres}}}
via

\boldsymbol{p}_{i}=\boldsymbol{p}_{i-1}-\eta\nabla F(\boldsymbol{p}_{i-1}).

When then take

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\left(\boldsymbol{p}_{t_{\text{thres}}},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\right),\text{ hence }\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\boldsymbol{p}_{t_{\text{thres}}},\mathcal{A}_{2}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}.

We then have

t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\begin{cases}t_{\text{thres}}&:\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|\leq g_{\text{thres}}\\
1&:\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|>g_{\text{thres}}.\end{cases}

We also define

\Delta(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\begin{cases}f_{\text{thres}}&:\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|\leq g_{\text{thres}}\\
\frac{\eta}{2}\cdot g_{\text{thres}}^{2}&:\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|>g_{\text{thres}}.\end{cases}

We now establish the crucial
Claim
2
: for all
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
,
\mathcal{A}
is a
(\mathcal{S},t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}),\Delta(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}),\frac{dL_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}}e^{-\chi},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
-decrease procedure. (Recall
\tilde{\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon}{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
.)

To do this, we use the following crucial Lemma ensuring high-probability decrease around saddle points
in the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set
:

Lemma D.2

(Equivalent of Lemma 13,
Jin et al. (2017)
)
.

There exists a universal constant
c_{\text{max}}\leq 1
such that the following occurs. Suppose we start with a
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, that is in the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set, satisfying the following conditions:

\left\|\nabla F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})\right\|\leq g_{\text{thres}}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{\min}(\nabla^{2}F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}))\leq-\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}.

Now let
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}+\boldsymbol{\zeta}
, where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is sampled uniformly from
\mathbb{B}(\vec{\boldsymbol{0}},r)
where
r
is defined in
Lemma
D.3
, and let
\{\boldsymbol{p}_{t}\}
be the iterates of gradient descent starting from
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}
. Then when the step size
\eta\leq\frac{c_{\text{max}}}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, with probability at least
1-\frac{dL_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}}e^{-\chi}
, we have:

F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t_{\text{thres}}})-F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})<-f_{\text{thres}}.

The variables in the above are defined in
Algorithm
1
. As noted earlier, because we work in the generalized smooth setting, the details require significant care compared to the proof of Lemma 13 in
Jin et al. (2017)
.

With
Lemma
D.2
, we have the ingredients to prove
Theorem
3.4
. First we establish
Claim
2
.

Proof of
Claim
2
.

We prove this by breaking into the following cases:

•

Suppose
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|>g_{\text{thres}}
. Then
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}=\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\eta\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
.

Our condition on
\eta
implies that

\eta\leq\frac{1}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}.

As
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, we have by
Corollary
1
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\right\|=\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|\leq\eta\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\leq\eta\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}.

Consequently, by
Lemma
3.1
,

F(\boldsymbol{p})\leq F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})+1\leq F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{p}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}}.

Now by
Lemma
A.1
and
Assumption
1.1
,

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{u}_{1})

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}+\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta^{2}}{2}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\frac{\eta}{2}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle<F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\frac{\eta}{2}\cdot g_{\text{thres}}^{2}=F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-\Delta(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}).

•

Else suppose
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right\|\leq g_{\text{thres}}
. Then
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
is perturbed, and we consider the sequence of the next
t_{\text{thres}}
iterates
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{p}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{p}_{t_{\text{thres}}}
.

Consider the event
\mathcal{E}
from
Lemma
D.2
, which occurs with probability at least
1-\frac{dL_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}}e^{-\chi}
.
Under
\mathcal{E}
, for such
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
, we have:

–

Either

F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t_{\text{thres}}})-F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})<-f_{\text{thres}},

that is

F(\boldsymbol{u}_{1})=F(\boldsymbol{p}_{t_{\text{thres}}})<F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})-f_{\text{thres}}.

–

Or

\lambda_{\textsc{min}}\left(\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\right)\geq-\sqrt{\tilde{\varepsilon}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})},\text{ hence }\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathcal{S}.

In all cases, by definition of
\mathcal{A}
, we conclude that
\mathcal{A}
is a
(\mathcal{S},t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}),\Delta(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}),\frac{dL_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}}e^{-\chi},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
decrease procedure for
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
.
∎

Consider these two cases, and recall the definition of
\overline{\Delta}
from
Theorem
2.1
. Using the definition of
\eta,g_{\text{thres}},f_{\text{thres}}
, we obtain for
c
a small enough universal constant,

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\frac{c^{2}\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2}}{2L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\chi^{4}},\frac{c^{3}\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2}}{\chi^{4}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c^{3}\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2}}{\chi^{4}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}.

Combining with
Theorem
2.1
, and note
t_{\text{oracle}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\leq t_{\text{thres}}\leq\frac{\max\left\{1,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\right\}}{\overline{\Delta}}
for
\tilde{\varepsilon}\leq 1
. We thus obtain the desired oracle complexity of
O\left(\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\max\left\{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),1\right\}\chi^{4}}{\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\max\left\{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),1\right\}\chi^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
to obtain an iterate in
\mathcal{S}
.
7
7

7

Note
t_{\text{thres}}
generally does not decrease with
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
, and this is why the
\max\left\{1,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\right\}
comes in.

We finally show the desired probability of success. Through
Theorem
2.1
, since
\chi\geq 18
and by definition of
\chi
, we can verify that the probability of failure is at most

\displaystyle\frac{dL_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}}e^{-\chi}\cdot\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}}\left\{\frac{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\Delta(\boldsymbol{w})}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{dL_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}}e^{-\chi}\cdot\frac{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\frac{c^{2}\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2}}{2\chi^{4}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}}}

\displaystyle\leq\chi^{4}e^{-\chi}\frac{2dL_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{c^{2}\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2.5}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\chi/4}\cdot\frac{2F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})dL^{2}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{c\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2.5}}

\displaystyle\leq\delta.

This completes the proof, assuming
Lemma
D.2
.
∎

D.2
Proving the key Lemma

We now prove
Lemma
D.2
to complete the proof. The rest of the proof is similar to that of
Jin et al. (2017)
, but hinges crucially on the fact that the analysis in
Jin et al. (2017)
is ‘local’.

Consider any
\gamma>0
, and define the ‘units’ in a similar way as
Jin et al. (2017)
, but now in terms of
L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})>0
defined earlier. First let the new ‘condition number’ be
\kappa=\kappa(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}):=\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\gamma}
(note this is
not
the real condition number, but rather is the ‘effective condition number’ of
\nabla^{2}F
in
\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
). Now define the following positive reals:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}

\displaystyle=\eta L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\frac{\gamma^{3}}{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}}\log^{-3}\left(\frac{d\kappa}{\delta}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\log\left(\frac{d\kappa}{\delta}\right)}{\eta\gamma},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\eta L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\log^{-2}\left(\frac{d\kappa}{\delta}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\eta L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\frac{\gamma}{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\log^{-1}\left(\frac{d\kappa}{\delta}\right).

Our goal is to prove the following.

Lemma D.3

(equivalent of Lemma 14 in
Jin et al. (2017)
)
.

There exists a universal constant
c_{\text{max}}
such that the following holds. For any
F
satisfying the conditions of
Theorem
3.4
, for any
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{d\kappa}{e}\right]
, suppose we start with a point
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
satisfying the following conditions for some
\gamma>0
, where
\mathcal{G}
is defined as above:

\|\nabla F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})\|\leq\mathcal{G}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{\min}(\nabla^{2}F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}))\leq-\gamma.

Let
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}+\boldsymbol{\zeta}
, where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is sampled from the uniform distribution over a ball with radius
\frac{\mathcal{L}}{\kappa\cdot\log\left(\frac{d\kappa}{\delta}\right)}:=r
and where
\mathcal{L}
is defined as above. Let
\{\boldsymbol{p}_{t}\}
be the iterates of gradient descent starting from
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}
. Then, when the step size
\eta\leq\frac{c_{\text{max}}}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have the following for any
T\geq\frac{1}{c_{\text{max}}}\mathcal{F}_{2}
:

F(\boldsymbol{p}_{T})-F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})<-\mathcal{F}_{1}.

Plugging in
\gamma=\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}
,
\eta=\frac{c_{\text{max}}}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
,
\delta=\frac{dL_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{\sqrt{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\tilde{\varepsilon}}}e^{-\chi}
into the above expressions for
\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L}
, using
c\leq c_{\text{max}}
, and directly applying
Lemma
D.3
, we immediately obtain
Lemma
D.2
. The rest of
Section
D
is thus devoted to proving
Lemma
D.3
.

Remark 9
.

Note it suffices to prove
Lemma
D.3
for
\delta
and
\gamma
smaller than universal constants, as the result
Theorem
3.4
will remain identical under the
O(\cdot)
. Thus we can assume WLOG that
\log(d\kappa/\delta)
is larger than some universal constant, and that
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{60}
. Also notice by our choice of step size
\eta\leq\frac{c_{\text{max}}}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
and the assumption
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{60}
, for
c\leq c_{\text{max}}\leq\frac{1}{12100}
we obtain

\kappa\geq 1,r\leq 1.

This in turn implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}

\displaystyle\geq 40,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\eta L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\cdot\frac{\gamma}{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}\cdot\log^{-1}\left(\frac{d\kappa}{\delta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{6600}\cdot\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)},\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}\right\},

where the second line uses that

L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\geq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\geq\max\left\{1,\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1),\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\right\}.

As
these assumptions come with no loss of generality
, we make these assumptions for the rest of the proof.

To show
Lemma
D.3
, again as in
Jin et al. (2017)
, we prove that the width of the stuck region is not too large.

Lemma D.4

(equivalent of Lemma 15 in
Jin et al. (2017)
)
.

There exists a universal constant
c_{\text{max}}
such that the following occurs. For any
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{d\kappa}{e}\right]
, let
F
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}
satisfy the conditions in
Lemma
D.3
. Without loss of generality, by rotational symmetry, let
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
be the minimum eigenvector of
\nabla^{2}F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})
. Consider two gradient descent sequences
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\}
and
\{\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\}
with initial points
\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
satisfying (again, denote the radius
r=\frac{\mathcal{L}}{\kappa\cdot\log\left(\frac{d\kappa}{\delta}\right)}
):

\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|\leq r,\quad\boldsymbol{x}_{0}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\pm\mu\cdot r\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\quad\mu\in\left[\frac{\delta}{2\sqrt{d}},1\right].

Then for any step size
\eta\leq\frac{c_{\text{max}}}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, and any
T\geq\frac{1}{c_{\text{max}}}\mathcal{F}_{2}
, we have:

\min\{F(\boldsymbol{u}_{T})-F(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}),F(\boldsymbol{x}_{T})-F(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\}\leq-2.5\mathcal{F}_{1}.

Now, we prove
Lemma
D.3
given
Lemma
D.4
.

Proof of
Lemma
D.3
given
Lemma
D.4
.

Recall as per
Remark
9
that

\left\|\boldsymbol{p}_{0}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|\leq r\leq\mathcal{L}\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)}.

Also recall
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
. Thus by
Lemma
D.1
we obtain for all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{p}_{0}\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}}
that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{u})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}

\displaystyle\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Therefore by
Lemma
A.1
,

F(\boldsymbol{p}_{0})\leq F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})+\left\|\nabla F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})\right\|r+\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{2}r^{2}\leq F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})+\mathcal{G}r+\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{2}r^{2}=F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})+\mathcal{F}_{1},

where we can readily verify from
Remark
9
that
\mathcal{G}r+\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{2}r^{2}\leq\mathcal{F}_{1}
.

Now let the stuck region be the set of points
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}
in
\mathbb{B}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}},r)
such that

F(\boldsymbol{p}_{T})-F(\boldsymbol{p}_{0})\geq-2.5\mathcal{F}_{1}.

Define the unstuck points by the complement of the stuck points.
