\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{w}^{1}(\boldsymbol{u}))<K_{o}|\mathfrak{F}^{1}\right)|\left\{\boldsymbol{w}^{1}(\boldsymbol{u})\in(\mathcal{S}^{B}_{K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0}))^{c}\right\}\right].

(56)

Conditioned on
\boldsymbol{w}^{1}(\boldsymbol{u})\leq(\mathcal{S}^{B}_{K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0}))^{c}
, we have by definition of
\mathcal{S}^{B}_{K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})
that
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{w}^{1}(\boldsymbol{u}))<K_{o}|\mathfrak{F}^{1}\right)\geq 0.4
. By
(
55
)
, for all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{w}^{1}(\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathcal{S}^{B}_{K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})^{c}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Thus combining with
(
56
)
implies for all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u})\leq K_{o}\right)\geq 0.4\cdot\frac{1}{2}=0.2.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:lowerboundprobescapeinball}

Now consider any
N^{\prime}\geq 1
. Notice as events,

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u})>N^{\prime}K_{o}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{w}^{(N^{\prime}-1)K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{u}))>K_{o}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{w}^{(N^{\prime}-1)K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{u}))>K_{o}\right\}\cap\left\{\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u})>(N^{\prime}-1)K_{o}\right\}.

Therefore,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u})>N^{\prime}K_{o}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{w}^{(N^{\prime}-1)K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{u}))>K_{o}|\mathfrak{F}^{K_{o}}\right)|\left\{\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u})>(N^{\prime}-1)K_{o}\right\}\right].

Note that conditioned on
\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u})>(N^{\prime}-1)K_{o}
, it follows that
\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{w}^{(N^{\prime}-1)K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{u}))\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
. Therefore
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{w}^{(N^{\prime}-1)K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{u}))>K_{o}|\mathfrak{F}^{K_{o}}\right)\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime})>K_{o}\right)
. Using
(
E.4
)
, we can upper bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u})>N^{\prime}K_{o}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u})>(N^{\prime}-1)K_{o}\right)\cdot\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime})>K_{o}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 0.8\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u})>(N^{\prime}-1)K_{o}\right).

(58)

Recall that
K_{0}=\lfloor\frac{\log(3/p)}{\log(0.8^{-1})}+1\rfloor K_{o}
. Let
N=\lfloor\frac{\log(3/p)}{\log(0.8^{-1})}+1\rfloor
. We obtain by repeatedly applying
(
58
)
for
N^{\prime}=N,N-1,\ldots
that

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u})>NK_{o}\right)\leq 0.8^{N}\leq p/3.

This gives the desired result.
∎

Now we prove
Lemma
E.13
.

Proof of
Lemma
E.13
.

Again, we proceed similarly as the proof of Lemma 8,
Fang et al. (2019)
. The main difference is we only have control over the relevant derivatives prior to the escape from
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
(recall
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
). However, it turns out that this is sufficient for the proof to go through.

Setup.

Recall that we have
\boldsymbol{w}^{0}(\boldsymbol{u})=\boldsymbol{u}
, and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}^{k}(\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})-\eta\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}^{k}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\eta\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}).

Now define the following stopping time:

\mathcal{K}_{1}=\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u})\land\mathcal{K}_{\text{exit}}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}).

For solely the purpose of analysis, consider the following sequence:

\boldsymbol{z}^{k}=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{w}^{k}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{k}(\boldsymbol{u})&:k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}\\
\left(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right)\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}&:k>\mathcal{K}_{1}\end{cases}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdescapesaddleszk}

Clearly the
\boldsymbol{z}^{k}
are
\mathfrak{F}^{k}
-measurable, because the event
\{k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}\}
is
\mathfrak{F}^{k}
-measurable.

Remark 20
.

Note unlike
Fang et al. (2019)
, the first case holds when
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
rather than
k<\mathcal{K}_{1}
. That being said we expect that if one uses the exact same definition as in
Fang et al. (2019)
for the
\boldsymbol{z}^{k}
, the proof this generalized smooth setting will still work, with a slightly modified argument compared to the proof we present.

Notice by definition of
\boldsymbol{w}^{0}(\boldsymbol{u}),\boldsymbol{w}^{0}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})
and assumption of
Lemma
E.13
that
\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
, we have
\mathcal{K}_{1}>0
. Thus,

\boldsymbol{z}^{0}=q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}.

Controlling the
\boldsymbol{z}^{k}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{H}=\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})
. We have the following lemma to control the
\boldsymbol{z}^{k}
from
(
E.4
)
.

For all
k
, define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}^{k}

\displaystyle:=\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})-\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}F\left(\boldsymbol{w}^{k}(\boldsymbol{u})+\theta(\boldsymbol{w}^{k}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{k}(\boldsymbol{u}))\right)\mathrm{d}\theta,

(60)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}

\displaystyle:=\left(\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}))-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}))\right)-\left(\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})-\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})\right).

(61)

Recall by definition of
\boldsymbol{w}^{k}(\boldsymbol{u})
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})

\displaystyle=\nabla f(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})+\tilde{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{k},

\displaystyle\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})

\displaystyle=\nabla f(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})+\tilde{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{k},

for the
same
noise sequence
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{k}
. Thus we also have

\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}=\left(\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}))-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}))\right)-\left(\nabla f(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})\right).\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:escapesaddlexidiff}

Lemma E.14

(Equivalent of Lemma 13,
Fang et al. (2019)
)
.

We have that for all
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
,

\boldsymbol{z}^{k}=(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H})\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}+\eta\boldsymbol{D}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}+\eta\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}.

Furthermore, we have the following properties of the
\boldsymbol{D}^{k}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}
defined in
(
60
)
,
(
61
)
:

1.

For all such
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{D}^{k-1}\right\|\leq L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\max\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|,\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|\right)\leq L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B.

2.

For all
k
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]=0.

3.

For all
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}\right\|\leq 2L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}\right\|.

Proof.
We prove each part one at a time:

1.

For
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
, using the definition of
\boldsymbol{z}^{k}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{k}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{w}^{k}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{k}(\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})-\eta\left(\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})-\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}-\eta\left(\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}))-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}))\right)

\displaystyle+\eta\left[\left(\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}))-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}))\right)-\left(\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})-\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}-\eta\left[\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}F\left(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})+\theta(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}))\right)\mathrm{d}\theta\right]\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}+\eta\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}-\eta\left(\boldsymbol{H}-\boldsymbol{D}^{k-1}\right)\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}+\eta\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}.

This proves the desired property of the
\boldsymbol{z}^{k}
.

2.

For the required properties of the
\boldsymbol{D}^{k-1}
, consider any
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
. First, notice
\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})+\theta(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}))=\theta\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})+(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})
for any
\theta\in[0,1]
. For
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
, both
\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}),\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
. Note this still remains true for
k=\mathcal{K}_{1}
because for
k-1=\mathcal{K}_{1}-1<\mathcal{K}_{1}
, the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{1}
implies that the iterates
\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}),\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
.

Thus for any
\theta\in[0,1]
,
\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})+\theta(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}))\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
, and so all points
\boldsymbol{p}
on the line segment between
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}
and
\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})+\theta(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}))
lie in
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
. Thus by
Lemma
E.8
,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{D}^{k-1}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})-\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})+\theta(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})))\mathrm{d}\theta\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})-\nabla^{2}F\left(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})+\theta(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}))\right)\right\|\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\leq L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\int_{0}^{1}\left\|\theta\left(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right)+(1-\theta)\left(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right)\right\|\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\leq L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\max\left\{\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|,\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|\right\}

\displaystyle\leq L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B.

The last line follows since
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
, hence
k-1<\mathcal{K}_{1}
, thus
\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}),\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
.

3.

Next as the stochastic gradient oracle
\nabla f(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\zeta})
is unbiased, applying Linearity of Expectation on
(
E.4
)
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]=0
for all
k
.

For the bound on the magnitude of
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}
, again recall by the above that for
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
, we have

\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}),\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B).

Thus for all
\boldsymbol{p}
on the line segment between
\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}),\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})
, we have
\boldsymbol{p}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
. Thus by
Lemma
E.7
,
\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{p})\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
. By
Lemma
E.9
, for any
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
,
\left\|\nabla^{2}f(\boldsymbol{p};\boldsymbol{\zeta})\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
. Recalling
(
E.4
)
gives

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}))-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}))\right\|+\left\|\nabla f(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u});\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{u})\right\|

\displaystyle=2L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}\right\|.

In the last step, we used the definition of
\boldsymbol{z}^{k}
for
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
.

This proves all the desired parts of
Lemma
E.14
.
∎

Controlling iterates under a high probability event.

We now consider a rescaled iteration as considered in
Fang et al. (2019)
. Recall the definition of
\delta_{m}\geq\delta_{2}
in the statement of
Lemma
E.13
. For each
k=0,1,\ldots
, we define:

\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}:=q^{-1}(1+\eta\delta_{m})^{-k}\boldsymbol{z}_{k}.

Lemma E.15

(Equivalent of the first part of Lemma 14,
Fang et al. (2019)
)
.

Define
\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}_{k}:=(1+\eta\delta_{m})^{-1}\boldsymbol{D}_{k}
, and slightly overloading notation, define

\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}:=q^{-1}(1+\eta\delta_{m})^{-k}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}.

Then for
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
, we have
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{0}=\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
and

\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}=\frac{\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}}{1+\eta\delta_{m}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}+\eta\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}+\eta\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d},

as well as the properties

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{k}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B\text{ for all }0\leq k<\mathcal{K}_{1},

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\text{ for all }1\leq k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}.

Proof.
We prove all the desired parts of
Lemma
E.15
.

•

The fact that
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{0}=\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
follows immediately, because
\boldsymbol{z}^{0}=q\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
. For the general recursion for
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}
, consider any
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
. First note that by the recursion for the
\boldsymbol{z}^{k}
for
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
in
Lemma
E.14
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}

\displaystyle=q^{-1}(1+\eta\delta_{m})^{-k}\boldsymbol{z}^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}}{1+\eta\delta_{m}}q^{-1}(1+\eta\delta_{m})^{-(k-1)}\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\eta\frac{\boldsymbol{D}^{k-1}}{1+\eta\delta_{m}}q^{-1}(1+\eta\delta_{m})^{-(k-1)}\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}+\eta q^{-1}(1+\eta\delta_{m})^{-k}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}}{1+\eta\delta_{m}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}+\eta\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}+\eta\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}.

•

Consider any
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
. For the requisite properties of
\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{k}
for
k<\mathcal{K}_{1}
, the upper bound on the norm of
\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{k}
follows immediately from
Lemma
E.14
.

Next from the definition of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}
and
Lemma
E.14
, for
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
we have that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq q^{-1}(1+\eta\delta_{m})^{-k}\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}_{d}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})q^{-1}\frac{(1+\eta\delta_{m})^{-(k-1)}}{1+\eta\delta_{m}}\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{k-1}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|.

This proves
Lemma
E.15
.
∎

Lemma E.16

(Equivalent of the rest of Lemma 14,
Fang et al. (2019)
)
.

With the step size
\eta
from
(
53
)
, there exists an event
\mathcal{H}_{o}
(namely, from
(
E.4
)
) with probability at least 0.9, such that for all
k\leq\min(\mathcal{K}_{1}-1,K_{0})
we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}\right\|^{2}\leq 4,\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdescapesaddleshighprobcontrol2event1}

and

\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}>\frac{1}{2}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdescapesaddleshighprobcontrol2event2}

Proof.
Define

\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1}=\frac{\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}}{1+\eta\delta_{m}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}.

Recall that
\boldsymbol{H}=\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})
and
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}
is in the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set
\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
. Therefore, from
Assumption
1.1
,
\left\|\boldsymbol{H}\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
. By definition of
\delta_{m}
, it follows that

-\delta_{m}\boldsymbol{I}\preceq\boldsymbol{H}\preceq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\boldsymbol{I}.

Since
\eta L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\leq 1
, it follows that the matrix
\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}
is symmetric and has all eigenvalues in
[0,1+\eta\delta_{m}]
. This implies

\left\|\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1}\right\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdescapesadleshatpsibound}

Note that
\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1}
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}
are measurable on
\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}
. This combined with
Lemma
E.14
and
Lemma
E.15
implies that for all
1\leq k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\cdot 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\leq 2}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]=1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\leq 2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}]=0,

and

|(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\cdot 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\leq 2}|^{2}\leq 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\leq 2}\cdot 4L^{2}_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|^{4}\leq(8L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))^{2}.

Now define the following real-valued stochastic process:

Y_{k}=(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\leq 2}1_{k-1<\mathcal{K}_{1}}=\begin{cases}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\cdot 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\leq 2}&:k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}\\
0&:k>\mathcal{K}_{1}.\end{cases}

Note
Y_{k}
is
\mathfrak{F}_{k}
-measurable, and that
(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1})^{\top},1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\leq 2},1_{k-1<\mathcal{K}_{1}}\equiv 1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}}
are all
\mathfrak{F}_{k-1}
-measurable. Thus, by
Lemma
E.14
and the definition of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}
from
Lemma
E.15
,

\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{k}|\mathfrak{F}_{k-1}\right]=0.

Furthermore combining the above justification with the trivial case
k>\mathcal{K}_{1}
, we obtain

|Y_{k}|\leq 8L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

By the (standard) Azuma’s Inequality, with probability
1-0.1/(2K_{0})
, for any given
l,1\leq l\leq K_{0}
:

\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{l}Y_{k}\right\rvert\leq 8L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sqrt{2l\log(40K_{0})}\leq 8L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sqrt{2K_{0}\log(40K_{0})}\leq\frac{1}{\eta},

where the last inequality follows from the given choice of parameters.

Analogously, by
Lemma
E.14
and
Lemma
E.15
, we also have for
1\leq k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\cdot 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\leq 2}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]=0,|\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\cdot 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\leq 2}|\leq 4L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Define

Y^{\prime}_{k}:=\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\cdot 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\leq 2}1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}}.

The (standard) Azuma’s Inequality now implies that with probability at least
1-0.1/(2K_{0})
, for any given
l,1\leq l\leq K_{0}
:

\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{l}Y^{\prime}_{k}\right\rvert\leq 4L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sqrt{2l\log(40K_{0})}\leq\frac{1}{4\eta}.

By the Union Bound, there exists an event
\mathcal{H}_{o}
happening with probability at least
0.9
such that the following inequalities hold for each
l=1,2,\ldots,K_{0}
:

\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{l}Y_{k}\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{\eta},\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{l}Y^{\prime}_{k}\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{4\eta}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:highprobevent_{e}scapesaddle}

In particular under the event
\mathcal{H}_{o}
, for any
l\leq\min(\mathcal{K}_{1}-1,K_{0})
, using the definitions of
Y_{k},Y^{\prime}_{k}
we obtain

\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{l}\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}_{k-1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\cdot 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|\leq 2}\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{\eta},\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{l}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\cdot 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k-1}\right\|\leq 2}\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{4\eta}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdescapesaddleshighprobeventimplication}

Now from
Lemma
E.15
, it follows for all
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}}{1+\eta\delta_{m}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}+\eta\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}+\eta\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1}\right\|^{2}+2\eta(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}_{k-1})^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}_{k-1}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k-1}+\eta^{2}\left\|\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}_{k-1}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}+\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\right\|^{2}+2\eta(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|^{2}+Q_{1,k}+Q_{2,k}+Q_{3,k}

(68)

where we define

Q_{1,k}:=2\eta(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1})^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1},Q_{2,k}:=\eta^{2}\left\|\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}_{k-1}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k-1}+\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{k}_{d}\right\|^{2},Q_{3,k}:=2\eta(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{k-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{d}^{k}.

For
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{1}
, we have
k-1<\mathcal{K}_{1}
. Thus by
Lemma
E.15
and
(
E.4
)
, we have

Q_{1,k}\leq 2\eta L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|^{2},\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdescapesaddlesQ1kbound}

and

\displaystyle Q_{2,k}

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta^{2}\left\|\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|^{2}+2\eta^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{d}^{k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta^{2}\cdot L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|^{2}+8\eta^{2}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 16\eta^{2}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k-1}\right\|^{2}.

(70)

The last inequality above follows as per
Remark
12
.

Now we complete the proof. Under the event
\mathcal{H}_{o}
from
(
E.4
)
, we prove
(
E.16
)
by induction on
k
(recall our condition for
k
for
Lemma
E.16
is that
0\leq k\leq\min(\mathcal{K}_{1}-1,K_{0})
).

When
k=0
, by
Lemma
E.15
,
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{0}=\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
, so
\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{0}\right\|=\left\|\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\right\|=1\leq 2
and
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{0}=\left\|\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\right\|^{2}=1
(recall
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
is a
unit
eigenvector), proving the base case.

Now for the inductive step, consider some
k\leq\min(\mathcal{K}_{1}-1,K_{0})
. Suppose
\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{l}\right\|\leq 2
holds for all
l,0\leq l\leq k-1
. Then because
k<\mathcal{K}_{1}
, upon applying the above bounds
(
68
)
,
(
E.4
)
,
(
70
)
we have:

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{0}\right\|^{2}+\sum_{s=1}^{k}Q_{1,s}+\sum_{s=1}^{k}Q_{2,s}+\sum_{s=1}^{k}Q_{3,s}

\displaystyle\leq 1+2\eta\sum_{s=1}^{k}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s-1}\right\|^{2}+16\eta^{2}L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\sum_{s=1}^{k}\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}\right\|^{2}+2\eta\sum_{s=1}^{k}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{s-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{d}^{s}

\displaystyle\leq 1+2L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B\cdot 4\cdot\eta k+16\eta^{2}\cdot L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\cdot 4\cdot k+2\eta\sum_{s=1}^{k}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}^{s-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{d}^{s}\cdot 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s-1}\right\|\leq 2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+16L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B\cdot\eta K_{0}+2\eta\sum_{s=1}^{k}{\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}_{s-1}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{d}^{s}\cdot 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s-1}\right\|\leq 2}\leq 1+1+2\eta\cdot\frac{1}{\eta}=4.

To upper bound the above, we used our choice of step size
\eta\leq\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B}{8L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}}
and
B\leq\frac{1}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
as per
Remark
12
, our above upper bounds on
Q_{1,s},Q_{2,s}
, and that the event
\mathcal{H}_{o}
implies
(
E.4
)
.

This completes the induction and proves
(
E.16
)
.

With
(
E.16
)
, we prove
(
E.16
)
. Namely note for
k\leq\min(\mathcal{K}_{1}-1,K_{0})
, summing and telescoping the recursion for
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}
from
Lemma
E.15
, we have:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0}+\sum_{s=0}^{k-1}\eta\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}_{s}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}+\sum_{s=0}^{k-1}\eta\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{d}^{s}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\eta\sum_{s=0}^{k-1}2L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}\right\|+\eta\sum_{s=0}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{d}^{s}\cdot 1_{\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s-1}\right\|\leq 2}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\eta\cdot K_{0}\cdot 2L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B\cdot 2+\eta\sum_{s=0}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{d}^{s}\cdot 1_{\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s-1}\|\leq 2}\geq 1-\frac{1}{8}-\frac{2}{8}\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Here to lower bound the final sum, we used that
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0}=\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
and the upper bound on
\left\|\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}_{s}\right\|
from
Lemma
E.15
, the fact that we have already established
\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}\right\|\leq 2
for all
s<k
as we showed
(
E.16
)
, and that the event
\mathcal{H}_{o}
implies
(
E.4
)
.

This proves all parts of
Lemma
E.16
.
∎

Finish.

Now we prove
Lemma
E.13
via the same high-level strategy as the proof of Lemma 8,
Fang et al. (2019)
. Note on the event
\{\mathcal{K}_{1}>K_{o}\}
, we have

\boldsymbol{z}^{K_{o}}=\boldsymbol{w}^{K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{w}^{K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{u})=(\boldsymbol{w}^{K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}))-(\boldsymbol{w}^{K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{u})-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}).

Thus by definition of
\mathcal{K}_{1}
, the event
\{\mathcal{K}_{1}>K_{o}\}
implies that

\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{K_{o}}\right\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{u}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{1})-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|+\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{K_{o}}(\boldsymbol{u})-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|\leq 2B.

That is,

\{\mathcal{K}_{1}>K_{o}\}\subseteq\{\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{K_{o}}\right\|\leq 2B\}.

However, consider the event
\mathcal{H}_{o}
,
(
E.4
)
from
Lemma
E.16
. On the event
\{\mathcal{K}_{1}>K_{o}\}\cap\mathcal{H}_{o}
, we have
K_{o}\leq\min(\mathcal{K}_{1}-1,K_{0})
, and so by
Lemma
E.16
, we have

\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{K_{o}}>\frac{1}{2}.

Thus by definition of
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}
and recalling
\delta_{m}\geq\delta_{2}>0
, on the event
\{\mathcal{K}_{1}>K_{o}\}\cap\mathcal{H}_{o}
we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{K_{o}}\right\|=q(1+\eta\delta_{m})^{K_{o}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{K_{o}}\right\|\geq q_{0}(1+\eta\delta_{2})^{K_{o}}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{K_{o}}\right\rvert>q_{0}\cdot\frac{6B}{q_{0}}\cdot\frac{1}{2}=3B,

where the last inequality uses
(
E.4
)
. This means that

\{\mathcal{K}_{1}>K_{o}\}\cap\mathcal{H}_{o}\subseteq\{\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{K_{o}}\right\|\geq 3B\}.

Putting our work together, we see that

\{\mathcal{K}_{1}>K_{o}\}\cap\mathcal{H}_{o}\subseteq\{\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{K_{o}}\right\|\geq 3B\}\cap\{\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{K_{o}}\right\|\leq 2B\}=\emptyset.

Therefore

\{\mathcal{K}_{1}>K_{o}\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{o}^{c}\implies\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{1}>K_{o}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{H}^{c}_{0})\leq 0.1.

Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{1}
, we conclude
Lemma
E.13
.
∎

Remark 21
.

Note we only have
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{T}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}>\frac{1}{2}
for
k<\mathcal{K}_{1}
due to the lack of global Lipschitz bounds on the graedient and Hessian of
F
, unlike in the proof of Lemma 8,
Fang et al. (2019)
.

E.5
Faster Descent

Setup:

As in
Subsection
E.4
, let
\mathcal{K}_{0}
denote the escape time of
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
for while loop of
Algorithm
2
when the while loop begins at
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}
. In this section, we aim to prove
Lemma
E.2
.

As in
Subsection
E.4
, the difference between
Lemma
E.2
and Proposition 9 of
Fang et al. (2019)
is that
this result only holds at points in the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set
\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
.
For the rest of this section, we work under the assumptions of
Lemma
E.2
; thus for the rest of this section,
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}
is in the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set
\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
.

The idea here is similar to that of
Subsection
E.4
. At a high level, we have the requisite control over the gradient and Hessian since the iterates we consider are in a neighborhood of a point
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
.
As in the previous part and as in
Fang et al. (2019)
, we let

\boldsymbol{H}:=\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0}),

and let

\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k+1}:=\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{x}^{k};\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k+1})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k}),\quad k\geq 0.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:vecxikdef}

Note as
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{k+1}
has mean 0 and as the stochastic gradient oracle is unbiased, we have that for all
k\geq 0
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k+1}|\mathfrak{F}^{k}\right]=0.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the subspace spanned by all eigenvectors of
\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})
whose eigenvalue is greater than
0
, and
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
denotes the complement space. Also, let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
denote the projection matrices onto the spaces
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
, respectively. Let
\boldsymbol{u}^{k}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{k}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0})
, and
\boldsymbol{v}^{k}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{k}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0})
. We can decompose the update equation of SGD as:

\boldsymbol{u}^{k+1}=\boldsymbol{u}^{k}-\eta\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\eta\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k+1},

\boldsymbol{v}^{k+1}=\boldsymbol{v}^{k}-\eta\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\eta\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k+1},

for
k\geq 0
. Clearly
\boldsymbol{u}^{0}=\boldsymbol{0}
,
\boldsymbol{v}^{0}=\boldsymbol{0}
.

Now decompose
\boldsymbol{H}=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\boldsymbol{U}^{T}
by the Spectral Theorem where
\boldsymbol{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is unitary and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is diagonal. Let
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{>0}
denote the diagonal matrix with diagonal entries equal to the positive (diagonal) entries of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\leq 0}
denote the diagonal matrix with diagonal entries equal to the zero or negative (diagonal) entries of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
. Now define

\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}:=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{>0}\boldsymbol{U}^{T},\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}:=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\leq 0}\boldsymbol{U}^{T}.

Thus
\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
has range in
\mathcal{S}
, and
\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}
has range in
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
. Note
\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}},\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}
are both symmetric.

From here, define the following quadratic approximations:

G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\left[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right]^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\boldsymbol{u},G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\left[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right]^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{v}.

Now define the quadratic approximation

G(\boldsymbol{x})=G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u})+G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v})\text{ where }\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}),\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}).

It is easy to see that

G(\boldsymbol{x})=\left[\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right]^{\top}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0})+\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}).

For convenience, let

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k}),\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k}).

Similarly, let

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{k}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}.

Also denote the stopping time

\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{0}\land K_{0}.

Due to its ‘local’ nature around the
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}
in the
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-sublevel set, we still have the following result from
Fang et al. (2019)
:

Lemma E.17

(Equivalent of Lemma 15,
Fang et al. (2019)
)
.

Consider any
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, and consider any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{u},B)
. Then we have

\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x})-\nabla G(\boldsymbol{x})\right\|\leq\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}}{2}.

Furthermore, for any symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
, with
0<a\leq\frac{1}{\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\right\|_{2}}
, for any
i=0,1,\ldots
, and
j=0,1,\ldots
, we have

\left\|(\boldsymbol{I}-a\boldsymbol{A})^{i}\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{I}-a\boldsymbol{A})^{j}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{a(i+j+1)}.

Proof.
Notice that for all
0\leq\theta\leq 1
,
\theta\boldsymbol{x}+(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{u},B)
. Thus as
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, by
Lemma
E.8
, we have

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\theta\boldsymbol{x}+(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{u})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{u})\right\|\leq L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\cdot\theta\left\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}\right\|\text{ for all }0\leq\theta\leq 1.

Thus we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x})-\nabla G(\boldsymbol{x})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{u})(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\left\{\int_{0}^{1}\left(\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0}+\theta(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}))-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{u})\right)\mathrm{d}\theta\right\}\left(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}\right)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{1}\left\{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\cdot\theta\left\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}\right\|\right\}\mathrm{d}\theta\right\|\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}}{2}.

The second part of the Lemma follows from the exact same proof of
Lemma
D.5
in
Section
D
. It is also proved in the proofs of Lemma 15,
Fang et al. (2019)
, and in the proof of Lemma 16 of
Jin et al. (2017)
. For more detail, let the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{A}
be
\{\lambda_{k}\}
. Thus for any
i,j\geq 0
, the eigenvalues of
(\boldsymbol{I}-a\boldsymbol{A})^{i}\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{I}-a\boldsymbol{A})^{j}
are
\{\lambda_{k}(1-a\lambda_{k})^{i+j}\}
. We now detail a calculation from
Jin et al. (2017)
. Letting
g_{t}(\lambda):=\lambda(1-a\lambda)^{t}
and setting its derivative to zero yields

\nabla g_{t}(\lambda)=(1-a\lambda)^{t}-ta\lambda(1-a\lambda)^{t-1}=0.

It is easy to check that
\lambda_{t}^{\star}=\frac{1}{(1+t)a}
is the unique maximizer, and
g_{t}(\lambda)
is monotonically increasing in
(-\infty,\lambda_{t}^{\star}]
.

This gives:

\left\|(\boldsymbol{I}-a\boldsymbol{A})^{i}\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{I}-a\boldsymbol{A})^{j}\right\|=\max_{k}\lambda_{i}(1-a\lambda_{k})^{i+j}\leq\hat{\lambda}(1-a\hat{\lambda})^{i+j}\leq\frac{1}{(1+i+j)a},

where
\hat{\lambda}=\min\{\ell,\lambda_{i+j}^{\star}\}
.
∎

Lemma E.18
.

For any
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}
, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Proof.
Note for
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}
, we have
k-1<\mathcal{K}_{0}
and so
\boldsymbol{x}^{k-1}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
. Recall furthermore that
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
. Thus, by
Lemma
E.5
and
Lemma
E.3
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|=\left\|\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{x}^{k-1};\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k-1})\right\|\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}),

as desired.
∎

Analyzing the Quadratic Approximation:

We now analyze the quadratic approximation
G(\boldsymbol{x})
as done in
Fang et al. (2019)
. First we analyze the part in
\mathcal{S}
:

Lemma E.19

(Equivalent of Lemma 16,
Fang et al. (2019)
)
.

Set hyperparameters from (8). With probability at least
1-p/4
, we have

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}})-G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{25\eta}{32}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}+4\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\left(\log(K_{0})+3\right)\log\left(\frac{48K_{0}}{p}\right)+\eta L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}K_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{25\eta}{32}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}+\tilde{O}(\varepsilon^{1.5}).

Proof.
We follow a similar strategy as before of combining the proof of
Fang et al. (2019)
with our self-bounding framework. To analyze
G_{\mathcal{S}}(\cdot)
we first consider an auxiliary Gradient Descent trajectory, which performs the update:

\boldsymbol{y}^{k+1}=\boldsymbol{y}^{k}-\eta\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k}),\quad k\geq 0,

and
\boldsymbol{y}^{0}=\boldsymbol{u}^{0}
.
\boldsymbol{y}^{k}
performs Gradient Descent on
G_{\mathcal{S}}(\cdot)
, which is deterministic given
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}
.

Noting
G_{\mathcal{S}}
has Hessian
\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
, and that
\boldsymbol{H}
is the Hessian of
F
at the point
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, we obtain from
Assumption
1.1
that

\left\|\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\right\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{H}\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Since the following only concern
G_{\mathcal{S}}
, then identically to the proof of Lemma 16,
Fang et al. (2019)
, we obtain the following:

•

By
L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-smoothness of
G_{\mathcal{S}}
(recall
G_{\mathcal{S}}
has Hessian
\boldsymbol{H}_{S}
), we obtain the so-called ‘Descent Lemma’:

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})+\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k}),\boldsymbol{y}^{k+1}-\boldsymbol{y}^{k}\rangle+\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{y}^{k+1}-\boldsymbol{y}^{k}\right\|^{2}.

\displaystyle=G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})-\eta\left(1-\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta}{2}\right)\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}.

•

Telescoping the above for
0\leq k\leq\mathcal{K}-1
, and by our choice of
\eta
which satisfies
\eta L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\leq\frac{1}{16}
as per
Remark
12
, we obtain

G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})\leq G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{0})-\frac{31\eta}{32}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:preliminarystep}

To obtain
Lemma
E.19
, we upper bound the difference between
\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}}
and
\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}}
. For all
k\geq 0
, define

\boldsymbol{z}^{k}:=\boldsymbol{u}^{k}-\boldsymbol{y}^{k}.

We aim to upper bound
\boldsymbol{z}^{\mathcal{K}}
(in an appropriate sense) using the concentration argument of
Fang et al. (2019)
:

Lemma E.20

(Equivalent of Lemma 17,
Fang et al. (2019)
)
.

With probability at least
1-p/6
, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{k}\right\|\leq\frac{3B}{32}\approx\tilde{\Theta}\left(\varepsilon^{0.5}\right),\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdfasterdescentconcentrationdiff1}

and

\boldsymbol{z}^{k^{\top}}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\boldsymbol{z}^{k}\leq 8\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\eta\left(\log(K_{0})+1\right)\log\left(\frac{48K_{0}}{p}\right)+\eta L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}K_{0}\approx\tilde{\Theta}\left(\varepsilon^{0.5}\right).\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdfasterdescentconcentrationdiff2}

Here
\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot)
hides
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-dependence.

Proof of
Lemma
E.20
.

Clearly
\boldsymbol{z}^{0}=\boldsymbol{0}
. From the definitions of
\boldsymbol{u}^{k},\boldsymbol{y}^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{k+1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{z}^{k}-\eta(\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k}))-\eta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{k}))-\eta\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{k+1}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})\boldsymbol{z}^{k}-\eta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{k}))-\eta\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{k+1},\quad k\geq 0.

(75)

Unraveling the above recursion gives:

\boldsymbol{z}^{k}=-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\eta(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{k-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}-\eta\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{k-1-j}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{j})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{j})),\quad k\geq 0.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:zrecursionsgdexpanded}

Setting
k=\mathcal{K}
, Triangle Inequality gives

\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{\mathcal{K}}\right\|\leq\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\eta(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right\|+\left\|\eta\sum_{j=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-1-j}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{j})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{j}))\right\|.

We separately bound these two terms:

•

For the first term, for any fixed
l
from
1
to
K_{0}
, and any
j
from
1
to
\min(l,\mathcal{K}_{0})
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\eta(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}|\mathfrak{F}^{j-1}\right]=0,\left\|\eta(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right\|\leq\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

The first equality uses
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right\|=\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{j}\right\|
and that the stochastic gradient oracle is unbiased. The inequality uses that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}
is a projection matrix,
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right\|=\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{j}\right\|\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
which follows as
j\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
Lemma
E.18
, and
\left\|(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j}\right\|\leq 1
which follows as
l\geq j
and
\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\succeq 0
. (Note the importance that
j\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}
, which gives us enough control over the noise term
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}
.)

Now to deal with the fact that the above control only applies for certain
j
, we define a stochastic process as follows, analogously to our proof of
Lemma
E.13
. For all fixed
1\leq l\leq K_{0}
, define a stochastic process
Y_{l,j}
over all
1\leq j\leq l
by:

Y_{l,j}=\eta(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}1_{j-1<\mathcal{K}}=\begin{cases}\eta(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}&:j\leq\mathcal{K}\\
0&:j>\mathcal{K}.\end{cases}

Recalling
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{0}\wedge K_{0}
, it’s easy to check that for any
fixed

l
,
Y_{l,j}
is
\mathfrak{F}^{j}
-measurable. Furthermore,
\eta(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j},1_{j-1<\mathcal{K}}
are both
\mathfrak{F}^{j-1}
-measurable. Thus combining with the earlier observations, we obtain that

\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{l,j}|\mathfrak{F}^{j-1}\right]=0,\left\|Y_{l,j}\right\|\leq\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Thus, by the Vector-Martingale Concentration Inequality
Theorem
C.1
, we have with probability
1-p/(12K_{0})
,

\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{l}Y_{l,j}\right\|\leq 2\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sqrt{l\log\left(\frac{48K_{0}}{p}\right)}\leq 2\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sqrt{K_{0}\log\left(\frac{48K_{0}}{p}\right)}\leq\frac{B}{16}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdfasterdescentconcentrationdiff1event1}

The last inequality uses our choice of parameters.

By a Union Bound, with probability at least
1-p/12
,
(
•
‣
E.5
)
holds for all
l
from
1
to
K_{0}
. In particular, with probability at least
1-p/12
we have for
\mathcal{K}
(recall
\mathcal{K}\leq K_{0}
) that

\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\eta(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right\|=\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{\mathcal{K}}Y_{\mathcal{K},j}\right\|\leq\frac{B}{16},

where we define
Y_{\mathcal{K},j}
the obvious way. This holds because with probability at least
1-p/12
, we have the bound
(
•
‣
E.5
)
on
\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{l}Y_{l,j}\right\|
irrespective of which value of
1\leq l\leq K_{0}
that
\mathcal{K}
takes on.
The first equality holds by our definition of
Y_{l,j}
for
j\leq l=\mathcal{K}
.

•

For the second term, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\eta\sum_{j=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-1-j}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{j})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{j}))\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\eta\sum_{j=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{j})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{j})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\eta\sum_{j=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{j})-\nabla G(\boldsymbol{x}^{j})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}K_{0}}{2}\leq\frac{B}{32}.

The first inequality uses the Triangle Inequality and that
\left\|(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-1-j}\right\|_{2}\leq 1
for
j
from
0
to
\mathcal{K}-1
; this follows because
\left\|\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
and as
\eta\leq\frac{1}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
. The second inequality uses
\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x})-\nabla G(\boldsymbol{x}))\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x})-\nabla G(\boldsymbol{x})\right\|
because
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a projection matrix. The third inequality follows from
Lemma
E.17
, and the fact that for all
j\leq\mathcal{K}-1
,
\boldsymbol{x}^{j}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
. The last inequality uses the choice of parameters.

Combining the above gives
(
E.20
)
, the first part of
Lemma
E.20
.

Now prove the second part of
Lemma
E.20
, namely
(
E.20
)
. Using the fact that
(\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b})^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b})\leq 2\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{a}+2\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{b}
for any symmetric positive definite matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
and the recursion
(
E.5
)
for
\boldsymbol{z}^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{z}^{\mathcal{K}}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\boldsymbol{z}^{\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta^{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-j-1}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{K-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{u}^{j}\right)

\displaystyle+2\eta^{2}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-1-j}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{j})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{j})\right)\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-1-j}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{j})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{j})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2\left\|\eta\sum_{j=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\eta^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\sum_{l=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{j})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{j})\right)^{\top}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-1-j}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-1-l}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{l})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{l})\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|\eta\sum_{j=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right\|^{2}+2\eta^{2}\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}}{4}\sum_{j=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\sum_{l=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-1-j}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-1-l}\right\|.

The last inequality follows by properties of projection matrices and by
Lemma
E.17
, recalling that for
j\leq\mathcal{K}-1
,
\boldsymbol{x}^{j}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
.

Now we bound each of these two terms separately:

•

For the first term, for any fixed
l,1\leq l\leq K_{0}
, again we define a stochastic process for any
j,1\leq j\leq l
by:

Y_{l,j}=\eta\left(\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right)1_{j-1<\mathcal{K}}=\begin{cases}\eta\left(\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right)&:j\leq\mathcal{K}\\
0&:j>\mathcal{K}.\end{cases}

Analogously to earlier, recalling
\mathcal{K}\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}
, for
fixed

l
, it is evident that
Y_{l,j}
is
\mathfrak{F}^{j}
-measurable,
\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j}1_{j-1<\mathcal{K}}
is
\mathfrak{F}^{j-1}
-measurable, and thus

\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{l,j}|\mathfrak{F}^{j-1}\right]=0.

We furthermore have

\left\|Y_{l,j}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}}{1+2(l-j)},

which follows by noting for any
1\leq l\leq K_{0}
and
j\leq\mathcal{K}\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\eta(\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right\|^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-j}\right\|\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}}{1+2(l-j)}.

This uses the second part of
Lemma
E.17
, that
\left\|\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
, that
j\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}
which gives
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right\|\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
by
Lemma
E.18
, and our choice of
\eta
(which cancels one of the
\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}
factors).

For a given
l
, by the Vector-Martingale Concentration Inequality
Theorem
C.1
, we have with probability
1-p/(12K_{0})
that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{l}Y_{l,j}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\log\left(\frac{48K_{0}}{p}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{l}\frac{1}{1+2(l-j)}

\displaystyle\leq 4\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}(\log(K_{0})+1)\log\left(\frac{48K_{0}}{p}\right).

(78)

The last step above uses
l\leq K_{0}
,
\sum_{j=1}^{l}\frac{1}{1+j}\leq\log(K_{0})+1
.

By the Union Bound, with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{12}
,
(
78
)
holds for all
l
from
1
to
K_{0}
. Because
1\leq\mathcal{K}\leq K_{0}
, using the definition of
Y_{l,j}
for
l\leq\mathcal{K}
, we obtain with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{12}
that

\eta\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{K-j}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{j}\right\|^{2}=\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{\mathcal{K}}Y_{\mathcal{K},j}\right\|^{2}\leq 4\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}(\log(K_{0})+1)\log\left(\frac{48K_{0}}{p}\right).

•

For the second term, using the second part of
Lemma
E.17
and that
\mathcal{K}\leq K_{0}
, and then rearranging order of the sum and performing explicit calculation yields

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}}{4}\sum_{j=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\sum_{l=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-1-j}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-1-l}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\eta\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}}{4}\sum_{j=0}^{K_{0}-1}\sum_{l=0}^{K_{0}-1}\frac{1}{1+j+l}

\displaystyle\leq\eta\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}}{4}\sum_{l=0}^{2(K_{0}-1)}\frac{\min(1+j,2K_{0}-1-j)}{1+j}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}K_{0}}{2}.

Combining the above two bounds proves
(
E.20
)
, the second part of
Lemma
E.20
.
∎

We introduce one more Lemma, an intermediate step in the proof of
Fang et al. (2019)
.

Lemma E.21
.

We have with probability at least
1-p/12
that

\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}}),\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}}\right\rangle\leq\frac{3\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}}{16}+8\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\log(48K_{0}/p)+\eta L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}K_{0}/2.

Proof of
Lemma
E.21
.

Let
\boldsymbol{y}^{*}=\arg\min_{\boldsymbol{y}}G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y})
; this exists as
G
is convex in the subspace
\mathcal{S}
, by the definition of
\mathcal{S}
. By the optimality condition of
\boldsymbol{y}^{*}
, we have:

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})=-\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\boldsymbol{y}^{*}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdconcentrationdifferenceinnerprodsgdoptcondition}

Let
\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k}=\boldsymbol{y}^{k}-\boldsymbol{y}^{*}
. From the update rule of
\boldsymbol{y}^{k}
and the optimality condition
(
E.5
)
, we obtain:

\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k}=\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k}),\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k+1}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:tildeyrecursion}

Consequently, using
(
E.5
)
and
(
E.5
)
, we have:

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}}),\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\mathcal{K}},\boldsymbol{z}^{\mathcal{K}}\right\rangle_{\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}}

\displaystyle=\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k-1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{u}^{k}\right\rangle_{\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-k+1}}-\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k},\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{k})\right\rangle_{\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-k}}.

Now we bound both of these sums in a manner similar to the proof of
Lemma
E.20
:

•

For the first term:
For any fixed
l
,
1\leq l\leq K_{0}
, define a real-valued stochastic process for any
k
,
1\leq k\leq\min(l,\mathcal{K}_{0})
by:

Y_{l,k}=\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k-1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{k}\rangle_{\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-k+1}}1_{k-1<\mathcal{K}}=\begin{cases}\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k-1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{k}\rangle_{\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-k+1}}&:k\leq\mathcal{K}\\
0&:k>\mathcal{K}.\end{cases}

Analogously to earlier, recalling
\mathcal{K}\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}
, it’s easy to check that for any
fixed

l
,
Y_{l,k}
is
\mathfrak{F}^{k}
measurable, and that all terms defining
Y_{l,k}
are
\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}
measurable except
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{k}
.
Thus,

\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{l,k}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]=0.

We furthermore have for any fixed
l,1\leq l\leq K_{0}
and
k,1\leq k\leq l
,

\left\|Y_{l,k}\right\|^{2}\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k-1})\right\|^{2}.

To justify why the above holds, clearly this is evident for
k>\mathcal{K}
. For
k\leq\mathcal{K}\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}
, note that

\displaystyle\left\lvert Y_{l,k}\right\rvert^{2}=\left\|\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k-1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{k}\rangle_{\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-k+1}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\left\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k-1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{k}\right\rangle\right\rvert_{(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-k+1}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{k}\right\rangle\right\rvert_{(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-k+1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k-1})\right\|^{2}\left\|(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{l-k+1}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k-1})\right\|^{2}.

Here we used that
\boldsymbol{H}_{S}
is symmetric, that
(
E.5
)
, that
\left\|\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l-k+1}\right\|\leq 1
which we have argued earlier in the proof of
Lemma
E.20
, and that
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{k}\right\|\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
as
k\leq l\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}
by
Lemma
E.18
and properties of projection matrices.

Now for any
l,1\leq l\leq K_{0}
, by the Azuma–Hoeffding inequality, we have with probability at least
1-p/(12K_{0})
that

\left\lvert\eta\sum_{k=1}^{l}Y_{l,k}\right\rvert\leq\sqrt{2\eta^{2}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\log(24K_{0}/p)\sum_{k=0}^{l-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}}.

Taking a Union Bound, it follows that with probability at least
1-p/12
, the above holds for all
l
with
1\leq l\leq K_{0}
.

Because
1\leq\mathcal{K}\leq K_{0}
always holds, using the definition of
Y_{l,k}
for
k\leq\mathcal{K}
, we obtain with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{12}
that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k-1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{k}\rangle_{\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-k+1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}Y_{\mathcal{K},k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\eta^{2}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\log(24K_{0}/p)\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta}{16}+8\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\log(48K_{0}/p)

where we used AM-GM in the last step. This holds because we have this upper bound on
\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{l}Y_{l,k}\right\rvert
irrespective of which value of
l,1\leq l\leq K_{0}
that
\mathcal{K}
takes on. The first equality holds by our definition of
Y_{l,k}
for
k\leq\mathcal{K}
.

•

For the second term: note

\displaystyle\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k},\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{k})\right\rangle_{\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{K-k}}

\displaystyle=\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}}),\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{k})\right\rangle_{(\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}})^{\mathcal{K}-k}}

\displaystyle\leq\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})\right\|\left\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{k})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})\right\|^{2}}{8}+2\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{k})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})\right\|^{2}}{8}+\frac{1}{2}\eta L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}K_{0}.

The first step above uses that
\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
is symmetric and
(
E.5
)
. The second step uses that
k\leq\mathcal{K}
and that
\left\|\boldsymbol{I}-\eta\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\right\|\leq 1
, as argued in the proof of
Lemma
E.20
. The third step uses AM-GM. The last step uses that
\mathcal{K}\leq K_{0}
and
Lemma
E.17
; for
k<\mathcal{K}
, we have
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
.

Combining these above two bounds proves
Lemma
E.21
.
∎

Now we finish the proof of
Lemma
E.19
. As done in
Fang et al. (2019)
, we combine
Lemma
E.20
,
Lemma
E.21
with
(
•
‣
E.5
)
to prove
Lemma
E.19
as follows. In particular, taking a Union Bound over the events from
Lemma
E.20
and
Lemma
E.21
, we obtain with probability at least
1-p/4
that

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}})

\displaystyle=G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})+\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}}),\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})

\displaystyle\leq G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})+\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}}),\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})

\displaystyle\leq G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})+\frac{3\eta}{16}\sum_{k=0}^{{\mathcal{K}}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+4\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}(\log(K_{0})+3)\log(48K_{0}/p)+L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\eta B^{4}K_{0}.

Here the first two lines used the definition of
G_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}
. The last line above applied
Lemma
E.21
together with the second part of
Lemma
E.20
.

Now combining the above with
(
•
‣
E.5
)
, we obtain

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}})

\displaystyle\leq G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}})+\frac{3\eta}{16}\sum_{k=0}^{{\mathcal{K}}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+4\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}(\log(K_{0})+3)\log(48K_{0}/p)+L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\eta B^{4}K_{0}

\displaystyle\leq G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})-\frac{25}{32}\sum_{k=0}^{{\mathcal{K}}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+4\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}(\log(K_{0})+3)\log(48K_{0}/p)+\eta L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}K_{0},

where we also used
\boldsymbol{y}^{0}=\boldsymbol{u}^{0}
. This proves
Lemma
E.19
.
∎

We now analyze the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
as in
Fang et al. (2019)
, where the analysis again goes through since the iterates are ‘local’, being prior to the escape time
\mathcal{K}
:

Lemma E.22

(Equivalent of Lemma 18,
Fang et al. (2019)
)
.

Deterministically, we have:

G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathcal{K}})\leq G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{0})-\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\eta\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathcal{K}-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle-\frac{7\eta}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}+L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}\eta K_{0}^{2}.

Note by choice of parameters that
L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}\eta K_{0}^{2}=\tilde{O}(\varepsilon^{1.5})
, where again the
\tilde{O}(\cdot)
hides
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
-dependence.

Proof.
By definition of
G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}
, and using definition of
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
which implies
\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\preceq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k+1}\right)

\displaystyle=G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right)+\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right),\boldsymbol{v}^{k+1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k+1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k+1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right)+\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right),\boldsymbol{v}^{k+1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right)-\eta\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right),\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k+1}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right)-\eta\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}-\left\langle\eta\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right),\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\eta\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k+1}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right)-\eta\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k+1}\right\rangle-\frac{7\eta}{8}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}+2\eta\left\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}\right)\right\|^{2}.

The last step uses AM-GM.

Substituting and telescoping the above for
k
from
0
to
\mathcal{K}-1
, we have:

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathcal{K}})

\displaystyle\leq G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{0})-\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\eta\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\rangle-\frac{7\eta}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}+2\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{0})-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\eta\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\rangle-\frac{7\eta}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}+\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}B^{4}\eta K_{0}}{2}.

Here, the second inequality uses that by
Lemma
E.17
, for all
k\leq\mathcal{K}-1
, we have
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
and so

\left\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|=\left\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{S}_{\perp}}(\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G(\boldsymbol{x}^{k}))\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla G(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|\leq\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}}{2}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Completing the Proof:

Now we have all the ingredients in hand to prove
Lemma
E.2
.

Proof of
Lemma
E.2
.

Again, we follow the strategy of
Fang et al. (2019)
and adapt it to our setting here where we do not have global bounds on the Lipschitz constants of the gradient and Hessian. With
Lemma
E.19
and
Lemma
E.22
in hand, the idea will be to show

\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}_{0}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}_{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}_{0}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}=\tilde{\Omega}(1),

and to bound the noise term

-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\eta\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\rangle.

We break the proof of
Lemma
E.2
into two cases:

1.

\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|>5\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
.

2.

\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|\leq 5\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
.

Case 1:

This case is more straightforward as the gradient is large, and will not use the quadratic approximation we developed earlier.

Consider any
k,0\leq k\leq\mathcal{K}-1
. Thus
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
, and so
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
for all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\boldsymbol{x}^{k}}
. By
Lemma
E.7
, as
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, we have
\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{u})\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
for all such
\boldsymbol{u}
. Thus as
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|>5\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
and by our choice of parameters,

\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|\geq\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|-\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|\geq 5\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})-L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B\geq\frac{9}{2}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:noisevsgradbound}

Similarly, as
\boldsymbol{x}^{k+1}=\boldsymbol{x}^{k}-\eta\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{x}^{k};\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k+1})
and again as
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, we have
\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{u})\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
for all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{x}^{k}\boldsymbol{x}^{k+1}}
by
Lemma
E.7
. Applying
Lemma
A.1
, for all
0\leq k\leq\mathcal{K}-1
, we obtain:

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k+1})-F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k}),\boldsymbol{x}^{k+1}-\boldsymbol{x}^{k}\right\rangle+\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{k+1}-\boldsymbol{x}^{k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}-\eta\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k}),\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k+1}\right\rangle+\frac{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta^{2}}{2}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})+\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k+1}\right\|^{2}.

\displaystyle\leq-\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}-\eta\left\langle\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k}),\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k+1}\right\rangle+L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta^{2}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}+L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k+1}\right\|^{2}.

\displaystyle\leq\eta\left(-\frac{15}{16}+\frac{5}{32}\right)\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}+\frac{8}{5}\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}+L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta^{2}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{25\eta}{32}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}+2\eta\sigma^{2}.

\displaystyle\leq-\eta\left(\frac{25}{32}-\frac{8}{81}\right)\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}.

Note here that we need to consider a bound on the Lipschitz constant of the gradient between
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}-1}
and
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}
; see
Remark
15
. Here, we used the update rule of SGD, AM-GM and Young’s Inequality, that
L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\eta\leq\frac{1}{16}
by our choice of hyperparameters,
Lemma
E.18
, and finally
(
E.5
)
in the last step.

Telescoping the above inequality from
k=0
to
\mathcal{K}-1
, we get:

F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}})-F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\leq-\eta\left(\frac{25}{32}-\frac{8}{81}\right)\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:easycaselowerboundgradsumpt0}

To upper bound the right hand side above, note by Triangle Inequality that

\displaystyle\left\|\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|-\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}+\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|-\left\|\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|.

(83)

By the Vector-Martingale Concentration Inequality
Theorem
C.1
and the bound
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
for all
k\leq\mathcal{K}
by
Lemma
E.5
, we obtain with probability at least
1-p/12
:

\left\|\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|=\left\|\eta\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}}\right\|\leq 2\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sqrt{K_{0}\log(48/p)}\leq\frac{B}{16}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:easycaselowerboundgradsumpt2}

Here, we used the fact that
1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}}\equiv 1_{k-1<\mathcal{K}}
and consequently
1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}}
is
\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}
-measurable, and that
\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]=0
,
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
for all
k\leq\mathcal{K}
.

Suppose the above event implying
(
E.5
)
occurs, which has probability at least
1-\frac{p}{12}
. Under this event, suppose that
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
is able to leave the ball
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in
K_{0}
iterations or less. If this is the case, then we have
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{0}\leq K_{0}
, and so
\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|\geq B
. Thus conditioned on the aforementioned event implying
(
E.5
)
, if
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
is able to leave the ball
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in
K_{0}
iterations or less, we obtain

\displaystyle\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{\eta\mathcal{K}}\left\|\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\eta\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{\eta\mathcal{K}}\left(B-\frac{1}{16}B\right)^{2}\geq\frac{15^{2}B^{2}}{16^{2}\eta\mathcal{K}}\geq\frac{15^{2}B^{2}}{16^{2}\eta K_{0}},

where we combined
(
83
)
,
(
E.5
)
to lower bound
\left\|\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\eta\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|
.
Here the first step holds by the elementary inequality
\left\|\sum_{i=0}^{l}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\right\|^{2}\leq l\sum_{i=0}^{l}\left\|\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\right\|^{2}
, and the last step uses
K_{0}\geq\mathcal{K}
.

Consequently by combining with
(
E.5
)
, with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{12}
, if
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
is able to leave the ball
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in
K_{0}
iterations or less, we have

F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}})\leq F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})-\left(\frac{25}{32}-\frac{8}{81}\right)\cdot\frac{15^{2}B^{2}}{16^{2}\eta K_{0}}<F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})-\frac{B^{2}}{7\eta K_{0}}.

Case 2:
Suppose
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|\leq 5\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
. To obtain the desired result, we first define and prove the following Lemmas. Proving these Lemmas in turn utilizes the Lemmas on quadratic approximation we have established earlier.

Lemma E.23
.

For all
0\leq k\leq\mathcal{K}-1
, we have

\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|\leq\frac{11}{2}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Proof.
By the condition in this case, properties of projection matrices, and as
\boldsymbol{v}^{0}=0
,

\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{0})\right\|=\left\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|\leq 5\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Note for
k\leq\mathcal{K}-1
, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{v}^{k}-\boldsymbol{v}^{0}\right\|=\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{k}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|\leq B.

Thus

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{0})\right\|+\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{0})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 5\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B

\displaystyle\leq\frac{11}{2}\sigma.

The above uses our choice of hyperparameters, and that
