\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{0})\right\|=\left\|\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k}-\boldsymbol{v}^{0})\right\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{H}\right\|\left\|\boldsymbol{v}^{k}-\boldsymbol{v}^{0}\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left\|\boldsymbol{v}^{k}-\boldsymbol{v}^{0}\right\|,

which in turn follows because
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
and by
Assumption
1.1
.
∎

The next Lemma is obtained by combining
Lemma
E.19
and
Lemma
E.22
, and it gives us a way to upper bound
F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})
.

Lemma E.24

(Equivalent of Lemma 19 in
Fang et al. (2019)
)
.

If
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|\leq 5\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
, with probability
1-\frac{p}{4}
, we have

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}})\leq

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})-\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle+\left(\frac{3}{256}+\frac{1}{80}\right)\frac{B^{2}}{\eta K_{0}}

\displaystyle-\frac{7\eta}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}-\frac{25\eta}{32}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}.

Proof.
For
k\leq\mathcal{K}-1
, we have
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
. Consequently the entire line segment
\overline{\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\boldsymbol{x}^{k}}
lies in
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
. As
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, by
Lemma
E.7
, we have

\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{k}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|\leq L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B.

Thus by our choice of parameters, as per
Remark
12
,

\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|+\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|\leq 5\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B\leq\frac{11}{2}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Recalling
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\mathcal{K}}\right\|\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
by
Lemma
E.18
, we obtain from our choice of parameters as per
Remark
12
that

\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{0}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\right\|+\eta\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}-1})+\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\mathcal{K}}\right\|\leq B+\frac{13}{2}\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\leq B+\frac{B}{100}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:hardcasesgdsecondorderbounddistance}

Using this, we then bound the difference between
F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}})
and
G(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}})
. As
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}=\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}-1}-\eta\nabla\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}-1};\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\mathcal{K}})
, as
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
, and as
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, we have
\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{u})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\right\|\leq L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left\|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|
for all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\overline{\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}}
by
Lemma
E.8
. Applying
Lemma
A.2
and recalling that
G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}})+G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathcal{K}})=G(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0})
, we obtain

F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}})-F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})-G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}})-G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathcal{K}})\leq\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}{6}\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|^{3}\leq\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{3}}{5}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdfasterdescenthardcasethirdorderbound1}

Here, we used
(
E.5
)
in the last step. Note here that we need to consider a bound on the Lipschitz constant of the Hessian between
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}-1}
and
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}
; see
Remark
15
.

Now, take a Union Bound over
Lemma
E.19
and
Lemma
E.22
. We now add the bounds from
Lemma
E.19
and
Lemma
E.22
to upper bound
G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}})+G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathcal{K}})
and use that
G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})+G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{0})=0
. Combining with
(
E.5
)
, we obtain with probability at least
1-p/4
that

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}})

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})-\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(v_{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{v}^{k}\right\rangle+4\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}(1+3\log(K_{0}))\log\left(\frac{48}{p}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{7\eta}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}-\frac{25\eta}{32}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}+\frac{3L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{4}\eta K_{0}}{2}+\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{3}}{5}.

(87)

Note by our choice of hyperparameters (analogous to the choice of hyperparameters from
Fang et al. (2019)
), we have the following bounds:

4\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}(1+3\log(K_{0}))\log\left(\frac{48}{p}\right)\leq\frac{B^{2}}{256\eta K_{0}},\frac{3L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{4}\eta K_{0}}{2}\leq\frac{B^{2}}{128\eta K_{0}},\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{3}}{5}\leq\frac{B^{2}}{80\eta K_{0}}.

Combining these above inequalities with
(
87
)
, with probability at least
1-p/4
, we obtain

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}})

\displaystyle\leq F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})-\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle+\left(\frac{3}{256}+\frac{1}{80}\right)\frac{B^{2}}{\eta K_{0}}

\displaystyle-\frac{7\eta}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{{\mathcal{K}}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}-\frac{25\eta}{32}\sum_{k=0}^{{\mathcal{K}}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}.

This implies
Lemma
E.24
.
∎

By
Lemma
E.24
, we want to lower bound the gradient norm of
G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}},G_{\mathcal{S}}
. We do this in the following Lemma, assuming
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
leaves the ball
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in
K_{0}
iterations.

Lemma E.25

(Equivalent of Lemma 20 in
Fang et al. (2019)
)
.

With probability
1-\frac{p}{6}
, if
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
exits
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in
K_{0}
iterations (i.e.
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{0}\leq K_{0}
), we have

\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}+\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}\geq\frac{169B^{2}}{512\eta K_{0}}.

Proof.
At a high level, the proof idea is similar to the proof of Case 1 earlier. Telescoping the recursions
\boldsymbol{v}^{k}=\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}-\eta\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}-\eta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})
and
\boldsymbol{y}^{k}=\boldsymbol{y}^{k-1}-\eta\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left(\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})+\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right)\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|-\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left(\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})+\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right)\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{v}^{0}+\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k+1}-\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right)+\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{0}\right\|

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{v}^{0}+\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k+1}+\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\right)-\left(\boldsymbol{z}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{z}^{0}\right)\right\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\left\|\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left(\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right)\right\|.

Here, we used that
\boldsymbol{z}^{k}=\boldsymbol{u}^{k}-\boldsymbol{y}^{k}
and the Triangle Inequality.

Next, recall
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}=\boldsymbol{u}^{k}+\boldsymbol{v}^{k}
for all
k\geq 0
, and
\boldsymbol{u}^{0}=\boldsymbol{v}^{0}=0
. Thus
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}=\boldsymbol{v}^{k}-\boldsymbol{v}^{0}+\boldsymbol{u}^{k}-\boldsymbol{u}^{0}
. Furthermore notice

\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})=\boldsymbol{H}_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}\left(\nabla G(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right).

For all
k\leq\mathcal{K}-1
we have
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
, so as
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
,
Lemma
E.17
gives

\left\|\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left(\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right)\right\|\leq\eta K_{0}\cdot\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}}{2}.

Applying these observations and Triangle Inequality again, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left(\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})+\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right)\right\|

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|-\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{z}^{0}\right\|-\eta\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\|-\frac{\eta K_{0}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|-\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{z}^{0}\right\|-\frac{B}{32}-\eta\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\|.

(88)

and
Lemma
E.17
combined with the fact that projection matrices do not increase norm and that
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
for
k<\mathcal{K}
, and the final statement is by the choice of hyperparameters.

Using
Lemma
E.20
and that
\boldsymbol{z}^{0}=0
, we obtain with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{12}
that

\left\|\boldsymbol{z}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{z}^{0}\right\|\leq\frac{3B}{32}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdfasterdescentlastlem2}

Now recall that
1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}}\equiv 1_{k-1<\mathcal{K}}
is
\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}
-measurable, which implies

\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}1_{\{k\leq\mathcal{K}\}}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]=\boldsymbol{0},

as the stochastic gradient oracle is unbiased. Furthermore, recall
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
for
k\leq\mathcal{K}
, and projection matrices do not increase norm.
Thus by the Vector-Martingale Concentration Inequality
Theorem
C.1
, with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{12}
, we have

\left\|\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\|=\left\|\eta\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}1_{\{k\leq\mathcal{K}\}}\right\|\leq 2\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sqrt{K_{0}\log\left(\frac{48}{p}\right)}\leq\frac{B}{16}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdfasterdescentlastlem3}

Thus taking a Union Bound over the events implying
(
E.5
)
,
(
E.5
)
and combining with the earlier display
(
88
)
, with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{6}
, we have

\left\|\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})+\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|\geq\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|-\frac{3B}{16}.

Thus with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{6}
, if
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
exits
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in
K_{0}
iterations (that is, if we have
K_{0}\geq\mathcal{K}
), we have

\left\|\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})+\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|\geq\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|-\frac{3B}{16}\geq B-\frac{3B}{16},

and so

\displaystyle\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}+\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2\eta\mathcal{K}}\left\|\eta\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left(\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})+\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2\eta\mathcal{K}}\left(B-\frac{3B}{16}\right)^{2}=\frac{169B^{2}}{512\eta\mathcal{K}}\geq\frac{169B^{2}}{512\eta K_{0}}.

In the first step above we used the elementary inequality
\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{l}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\right\|^{2}\leq l\sum_{i=1}^{l}\left\|\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\right\|^{2}
and Young’s Inequality. This proves
Lemma
E.25
.
∎

We now combine
Lemma
E.24
,
Lemma
E.25
to prove
Lemma
E.2
. First recall by
Lemma
E.24
, with probability
1-p/4
, we have

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}})\leq

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})-\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle+\left(\frac{3}{256}+\frac{1}{80}\right)\frac{B^{2}}{\eta K_{0}}

\displaystyle-\frac{7\eta}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}-\frac{25\eta}{32}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}.

(91)

We first control
\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle
by concentration. For all
k
from
1
to
K_{0}
, note

\mathbb{E}\left[\eta\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle 1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k-1}\right]=0,

because
1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}}\equiv 1_{k-1\leq\mathcal{K}}
, so all terms in
\eta\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle 1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}}
except
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}
are
\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}
-measurable.

Furthermore, by
Lemma
E.23
and
Lemma
E.18
, for all
k\leq\mathcal{K}
, we have

\left\|\eta\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle 1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}}\right\|\leq\frac{11\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}}{2},

and

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{\eta\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle 1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}}\right\}^{2}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]\leq\eta^{2}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}1_{k\leq K}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}.

Taking
\delta=\frac{p}{3\log(K_{0})}
in the Data-Dependent Bernstein Inequality
Theorem
C.2
, we obtain with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{3}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}-\eta\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}-\eta\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle 1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{11\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\log\left(\frac{3\log(K_{0})}{p}\right),4\sqrt{\eta^{2}\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}\log\left(\frac{3\log(K_{0})}{p}\right)}\right\}.

(92)

We upper bound each of these terms in the maximum. With our choice of parameters and one application of AM-GM, we have

11\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}\log\left(\frac{3\log(K_{0})}{p}\right)\leq\frac{B^{2}}{100\eta K_{0}},

and

\displaystyle 4\sqrt{\eta^{2}\sigma^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}\log\left(\frac{3\log(K_{0})}{p}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 32\log\left(\frac{3\log(K_{0})}{p}\right)\eta\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{B^{2}}{32\eta K_{0}}+\frac{\eta}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}.

Consequently the second upper bound dominates the maximum from
(
92
)
. Substituting the above into
(
92
)
, with probability at least
1-\frac{p}{3}
, we obtain

\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}-\eta\left\langle\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k-1}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{k}\right\rangle\leq\frac{B^{2}}{32\eta K_{0}}+\frac{\eta}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}.

Combining with
(
91
)
, we obtain with probability at least
1-\frac{7p}{12}
that

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}})-F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{3}{256}+\frac{1}{80}+\frac{1}{32}\right)\frac{B^{2}}{\eta K_{0}}-\frac{3\eta}{4}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{k})\right\|^{2}-\frac{3\eta}{4}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathcal{K}-1}\left\|\nabla G_{\mathcal{S}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}

Taking a Union Bound with the event from
Lemma
E.25
, we obtain with probability at least
1-\frac{3}{4}p
, if
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
moves out of the ball
\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
within
K_{0}
iterations (i.e.
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{0}\leq K_{0}
), then

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}_{0}})-F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})

\displaystyle=F(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathcal{K}})-F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0})\leq-\left(\frac{3}{4}\cdot\frac{169}{512}-\frac{3}{256}-\frac{1}{80}-\frac{1}{32}\right)\frac{B^{2}}{\eta K_{0}}<-\frac{B^{2}}{7\eta K_{0}}.

This proves
Lemma
E.2
in Case 2.

Combining Case 1 and Case 2, we obtain
Lemma
E.2
.
∎

E.6
Finding Second Order Stationary Points

Here, we finish the proof by showing with high probability, if the algorithm does not escape
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in
K_{0}
iterates, then the average of the
K_{0}
iterates is a SOSP. In particular, we aim to prove
Lemma
E.1
. Here is where
Lemma
E.12
is used. In the following, we define
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}
as in
(
E.5
)
.
Furthermore, note the proofs of
Lemma
E.17
and
Lemma
E.18
still go through under the conditions of
Lemma
E.1
, so we may apply those Lemmas in our proof here.

Proof.
We adopt the proof strategy of
Fang et al. (2019)
in a similar way as we have thus far.

•

By
Lemma
E.12
, with probability
1-\frac{p}{3}
(namely if the event
(
E.4
)
from
Lemma
E.12
occurs), then if
\lambda_{\textsc{min}}(\nabla^{2}F(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}))\leq-\delta_{2}
,
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
will move out of the ball
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
within
K_{0}
iterations. By taking the contrapositive, we see that with probability
1-\frac{p}{3}
, if
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
does not move out of the ball
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in
K_{0}
iterations, then
\lambda_{\textsc{min}}(\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0}))\geq-\delta_{2}
. In this case, we have
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
for all
1\leq k\leq K_{0}
, so
\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
. Thus by
Lemma
E.8
and as
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
,

\lambda_{\textsc{min}}(\nabla^{2}F(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}))\geq\lambda_{\textsc{min}}(\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{x}^{0}))-L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\left\|\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\right\|\geq-\delta_{2}-L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B\geq-17\delta,

where the final inequality follows from our choice of parameters. That is, with probability
1-\frac{p}{3}
, if
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
does not move out of the ball
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in
K_{0}
iterations, then
\lambda_{\textsc{min}}(\nabla^{2}F(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}))\geq-17\delta
.

•

To complete the proof and show
\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}
is a SOSP, we will show that
\left\|\nabla F(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}})\right\|
is small. To this end, we upper bound
\frac{1}{K_{0}}\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|
using concentration. In deriving this bound we do
not
yet suppose that
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
does not move out of
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in its first
K_{0}
iterations. Consider

\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}}\right\|=\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}1_{k-1<\mathcal{K}_{0}}\right\|.

As
1_{k-1<\mathcal{K}_{0}}
is
\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}
-measurable,

\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}}|\mathfrak{F}^{k-1}\right]=\boldsymbol{0}.

Furthermore by
Lemma
E.18
, for
k\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}
we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}}\right\|\leq\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}).

Thus the Vector-Martingale Concentration Inequality
Theorem
C.1
gives with probability at least
1-2p/3
that

\frac{1}{K_{0}}\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}}\right\|\leq\frac{2\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})\sqrt{K_{0}\log(6/p)}}{K_{0}}\leq L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:restartedsgdfindingsospeq1}

The last inequality follows from our choice of parameters.

Now conditioning on the above event implying
(
•
‣
E.6
)
which occurs with probability at least
1-2p/3
, suppose
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
does not move out of the ball
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in
K_{0}
iterations. Then we have
\mathcal{K}_{0}>K_{0}
, and so from
(
•
‣
E.6
)
, we have

\frac{1}{K_{0}}\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|=\frac{1}{K_{0}}\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}1_{k\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}}\right\|\leq L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}.

Furthermore, if
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
does not move out of the ball
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
in
K_{0}
iterations, then we have
\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
. We find an upper bound
\left\|\nabla F(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}})\right\|^{2}
. We again consider the quadratic approximation
G(\boldsymbol{x})
at
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}
defined in
Subsection
E.5
, and follow the notation from there. Noting
G(\cdot)
is a quadratic and so its gradient is a linear map, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|G(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{K_{0}}\sum_{k=0}^{K_{0}-1}\nabla G(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{1}{K_{0}}\sum_{k=0}^{K_{0}-1}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|+\left\|\frac{1}{K_{0}}\sum_{k=0}^{K_{0}-1}\nabla G(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K_{0}\eta}\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{K_{0}-1}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}-\eta\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|+\left\|\frac{1}{K_{0}}\sum_{k=0}^{K_{0}-1}\nabla G(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})-\nabla F(\boldsymbol{x}^{k})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{B}{K_{0}\eta}+\frac{1}{K_{0}}\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|+\frac{1}{K_{0}}\cdot K_{0}\cdot\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{16}{\tilde{C_{1}}}+\frac{1}{2}\right)L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}+\frac{1}{K_{0}}\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|.

Here we used the choice of parameters, that
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
for all
0\leq k\leq K_{0}
combined with
Lemma
E.17
and that
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
, and Triangle Inequality repeatedly.

Note because
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{F,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
and as
\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
, by
Lemma
E.17
, the above implies

\left\|\nabla F(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}})\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla G(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}})\right\|+\frac{L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}}{2}\leq 17L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}+\frac{1}{K_{0}}\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{k}\right\|\leq 18L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}.

Consequently, with probability at least
1-2p/3
, if
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
does not move out of the ball
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
within
K_{0}
iterations, then

\left\|\nabla F(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}})\right\|\leq 18L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2}.

Taking a Union Bound, it follows that with probability at least
1-p
, if
\boldsymbol{x}^{k}
does not escape
\mathbb{B}(\boldsymbol{x}^{0},B)
within the first
K_{0}
iterations, we have both

\left\|\nabla F(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}})\right\|\leq 18L_{2}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})B^{2},\lambda_{\textsc{min}}(\nabla^{2}F(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}))\geq-17\delta.

This proves
Lemma
E.1
.
∎

Appendix F
Examples

F.1
Phase Retrieval

By
Theorem
3.4
and
Theorem
3.5
, it suffices to show that 1)
F_{\text{pr}}
satisfies
Assumption
1.2
and 2)
F_{\text{pr}}
is a strict saddle problem (that is, all SOSPs are near-optima in a suitable sense). In the rest of this subsection, denote
F_{\text{pr}}
by
F
for short. As shown in
Candes et al. (2015)
;
De Sa et al. (2022)
, Section 2.3 and Lemma 16 part a respectively, direct calculation shows
F(\boldsymbol{w})
takes the form

F(\boldsymbol{w})=\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{I}-(\boldsymbol{w}^{\star})(\boldsymbol{w}^{\star})^{\top})\boldsymbol{w}+\frac{3}{4}(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1)^{2}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:phaseretrievalloss}

As
\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\|=1
, we have
F(\boldsymbol{w})\geq 0
. Furthermore, we have
\inf_{\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(\boldsymbol{w})=0
, attained for example at
\boldsymbol{w}=\pm\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}
. Also note for any fixed
\boldsymbol{w}
,
F
is absolutely continuous on a compact neighborhood of
\boldsymbol{w}
.

F
satisfies
Assumption
1.2
:

By
De Sa et al. (2022)
, Lemma 20, we have that

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq\rho_{1}(F(\boldsymbol{w}))

for
\rho_{1}(x)=9\sqrt{x}+10
. It remains to show that

\left\|\nabla^{3}F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq\rho_{2}(F(\boldsymbol{w}))

for some increasing, non-negative
\rho_{2}
, where
\left\|\nabla^{3}F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|
refers to operator norm of the third order tensor. Equivalently, we will show that for any
\boldsymbol{w}
and any unit vector
\boldsymbol{u}
, we have

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}}{\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|}\leq\rho_{2}(F(\boldsymbol{w})).

As shown in the proof of Lemma 20,
De Sa et al. (2022)
, we obtain from direct calculation that

\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})=2\boldsymbol{I}-2(\boldsymbol{w}^{\star})(\boldsymbol{w}^{\star})^{\top}+3(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1)\boldsymbol{I}+6\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:phaseretrievalhessian}

Thus, by repeatedly applying Triangle Inequality and
Lemma
A.3
and as
\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|=1
,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}

\displaystyle=\left\|3(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\|^{2}-\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2})\boldsymbol{I}+6(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}-6\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\left\lvert\left\|\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\|-\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|\right\rvert\cdot\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\|+\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+6\left\|(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}-\boldsymbol{w}(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}+\boldsymbol{w}(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}-\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|\left(2\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+\delta\right)+6\left(\left\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}+\left\|\boldsymbol{w}(\delta\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|\left(3\left(2\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+\delta\right)+6\left\|\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\|+6\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|\left(18\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+9\delta\right).

Here, we used the inequality
\left\lvert\left\|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}\right\|-\left\|\boldsymbol{x}\right\|\right\rvert\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{y}\right\|
.

Consequently,

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}}{\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|}\leq\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}18\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+9\delta\leq 18\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+1.

By Lemma 16 part d,
De Sa et al. (2022)
, using Jensen’s Inequality we have

F(\boldsymbol{w})\geq(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1)^{2}.

Note for
\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|\geq 2
, this implies

18\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+1\leq 18\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+1\right)^{2}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|-1\right)^{2}\leq 18F(\boldsymbol{w}).

Combining with the case
\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|<2
, we obtain

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}}{\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|}\leq 18\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+1\leq 18F(\boldsymbol{w})+37,

so we can just take
\rho_{2}(x)=18x+37
.

Next, we check that
F
is a strict saddle problem:

We check this here. Similar results, in slightly different of a setting where we solve phase retrieval from samples from data, are shown in
Sun et al. (2018)
.

Suppose
\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\leq\delta
for
\delta\leq(\frac{1}{20})^{4}
. Note by Lemma 16 part b,
De Sa et al. (2022)
,
\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}^{\star},\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\rangle=3(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1)\left\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\rangle
. By Cauchy-Schwartz and recalling
\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}
is a unit vector, this gives

\delta\geq\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\|\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|\geq\left\lvert\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}^{\star},\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\rangle\right\rvert=3\left\lvert\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1\right\rvert\cdot\left\lvert\left\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\rangle\right\rvert.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:phaseretrievalprovesseq}

•

Suppose
\left\lvert\left\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\rangle\right\rvert\geq\sqrt{\delta}
. Combining this with
(
F.1
)
gives

\left\lvert\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1\right\rvert\leq\frac{\sqrt{\delta}}{3}.

By Lemma 16 part c,
De Sa et al. (2022)
,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=12\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})-8(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-\left\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\rangle^{2})

\displaystyle=(12\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-8)F(\boldsymbol{w})+6(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1)^{2},

where the last equality follows from the explicit form
F(\boldsymbol{w})
from
(
F.1
)
. Thus using
\left\lvert\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1\right\rvert\leq\frac{\sqrt{\delta}}{3}
, we obtain

\delta^{2}\geq\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|^{2}=(12\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-8)F(\boldsymbol{w})+6(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1)^{2}\geq(4-4\sqrt{\delta})F(\boldsymbol{w}).

For
\delta\leq\frac{1}{4}
, this gives

F(\boldsymbol{w})\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{4-4\sqrt{\delta}}\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}.

•

Otherwise, suppose
\left\lvert\left\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq\sqrt{\delta}
. Note by differentiating
(
F.1
)
, as shown in the proof of Lemma 16 part b,
De Sa et al. (2022)
,

\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})=2\boldsymbol{w}-2\left\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\rangle\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}+3(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1)\boldsymbol{w}=-2\left\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\rangle\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}+(3\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1)\boldsymbol{w}.

Thus by Triangle Inequality,

\left\lvert 3\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1\right\rvert\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|+2\left\lvert\left\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\rangle\right\rvert\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\|\leq\delta+2\sqrt{\delta}\leq 4\sqrt{\delta}.

Consequently either
\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|\leq 2\delta^{1/4}
or
\left\lvert 3\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1\right\rvert\leq 2\delta^{1/4}
.

In the first case, by Cauchy Schwartz and
(
F.1
)
, notice for any unit vector
\boldsymbol{u}
that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\left(2\boldsymbol{I}-2(\boldsymbol{w}^{\star})(\boldsymbol{w}^{\star})^{\top}+3(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1)\boldsymbol{I}+6\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{T}\right)]\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle\leq-\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|^{2}+3\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|^{2}\cdot(2\delta^{1/4})^{2}+6\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|^{2}\cdot(2\delta^{1/4})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-1+36\delta^{1/2}\leq-\frac{9}{10},

since
\delta\leq(\frac{1}{20})^{4}
.

In the second case, using
(
F.1
)
, notice as
\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\|=1
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}^{\star^{\top}}\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{w}^{\star^{\top}}(3\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}-1)\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}-2\left\|\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\|^{2}+6\left\lvert\left\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\star}\right\rangle\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta^{1/4}-2+6\delta\leq-\frac{9}{5}.

Consequently in either case,
\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})
has at least one negative eigenvalue with value at most
-\frac{9}{10}
.

Consider
\varepsilon
smaller than a universal constant, and take
\delta=\sqrt{\varepsilon}
in the above result. It follows from the analysis here that if we find an SOSP to tolerance
\varepsilon
as per the definition
(
1
)
, we obtain
\boldsymbol{w}
with
F(\boldsymbol{w})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
.

Thus, it follows that running Perturbed GD or Restarted SGD as described in
Theorem
3.4
or
Theorem
3.5
respectively, we will obtain
\boldsymbol{w}
with suboptimality
F(\boldsymbol{w})\leq\varepsilon
, where the number of oracle calls depends on
1/\varepsilon,d,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
in the same way as in
Theorem
3.4
or
Theorem
3.5
respectively.

F.2
Matrix PCA

Again by
Theorem
3.4
,
Theorem
3.5
, it suffices to show that 1)
F_{\text{pca}}
satisfies
Assumption
1.2
and 2) is a strict saddle problem (that is, all SOSPs are near-optima in a suitable sense). We will show this, with the parameters governing the strict saddle property depending on the spectral gap

\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{M})-\lambda_{2}(\boldsymbol{M})
.
11
11

11

Thus our result will be vacuous when the spectral gap is 0.
In the rest of this subsection, denote
F_{\text{pca}}
by
F
for short. Recall the loss function for PCA takes the form

F(\boldsymbol{w})=\frac{1}{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{M}\right\|_{F}^{2},

where
\boldsymbol{M}
is a symmetric PD matrix. Note for any fixed
\boldsymbol{w}
,
F
is absolutely continuous on a compact neighborhood of
\boldsymbol{w}
. Note
F(\boldsymbol{w})\geq 0
always holds. While it is not true that
\inf_{\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(\boldsymbol{w})=0
, to enforce this, we can consider the shifted function
G:=F-\inf_{\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(\boldsymbol{w})
. The derivatives of
G
are identical to those of
F
, and furthermore
G(\boldsymbol{x})-G(\boldsymbol{y})=F(\boldsymbol{x})-F(\boldsymbol{y})
for all
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}
. Thus to apply
Theorem
3.4
,
Theorem
3.5
and show that Perturbed GD or Restarted SGD can globally optimize
G
and therefore
F
by finding SOSPs, it remains to show
F
satisfies
Assumption
1.2
and is strict saddle.

F
satisfies
Assumption
1.2
:

Direct calculation, also in
Jin et al. (2021a)
, yields

\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})=(\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{M})\boldsymbol{w},\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})=\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}\boldsymbol{I}+2\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{M}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:gradhessianpcacalc}

We now check self-bounding regularity for the Hessian and third order derivative tensor. First observe

\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top})\boldsymbol{w}=\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{4}.

Combining with
Lemma
A.3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}+\left\|\boldsymbol{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{M}\right\|_{F}^{1/2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 2F(\boldsymbol{w})^{1/4}+\left\|\boldsymbol{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}^{1/2}.

(98)

Now we check the self bounding conditions.
For the Hessian, note from
(
F.2
)
and
(
98
)
and using
Lemma
A.3
,

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq 3\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq 3(2F(\boldsymbol{w})^{1/4}+\left\|\boldsymbol{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}^{1/2})^{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}.

Thus we can take
\rho_{1}(x)=3(2x^{1/4}+\left\|\boldsymbol{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}^{1/2})^{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}
.

For the third order derivative tensor, following the strategy in
Subsection
F.1
, we will show that for any
\boldsymbol{w}
and any unit vector
\boldsymbol{u}
, we have

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}}{\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|}\leq\rho_{3}(F(\boldsymbol{w})).

Applying
(
F.2
)
and
Lemma
A.3
and note

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}-\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}-(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}+(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})(\delta\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}+\delta\boldsymbol{u}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}.

This gives

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\left\|\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})-\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}}{\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{(\left\|\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\|^{2}-\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2})+2\left\|(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})(\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}-\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}}{\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\left\lvert\left\|\boldsymbol{w}+\delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\|-\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|\right\rvert\cdot(2\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|)+\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|(2\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|)}{\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|(2\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|)+\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|(2\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|)}{\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}4\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|+2\delta\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\right\|

\displaystyle=4\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 8F(\boldsymbol{w})^{1/4}+4\left\|\boldsymbol{M}\right\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{op}{}}.

Here we used the inequality
\left\lvert\left\|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}\right\|-\left\|\boldsymbol{x}\right\|\right\rvert\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{y}\right\|
. The last step used
(
98
)
. Thus we can take
\rho_{2}(x)=8{x}^{1/4}+4\left\|\boldsymbol{M}\right\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{op}{}}
.

Next, we check
F
is a strict saddle problem:

We check this here. A similar verification is done in
Ge et al. (2017)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{v}_{d}
be the (unit) eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{M}
corresponding to
\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{M})\geq\lambda_{2}(\boldsymbol{M})\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{d}(\boldsymbol{M})>0
respectively (recall
\boldsymbol{M}
is assumed to be PD). Thus the
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}
form an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Furthermore for convenience let
\lambda_{i}:=\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{M})
for all
1\leq i\leq d
. As
\boldsymbol{M}
is symmetric and PD, by the Spectral Theorem, we can write

\boldsymbol{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}^{\top}.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{w}
is a SOSP to tolerance
\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon<\min\left\{1,\frac{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}}{16},\frac{3}{8}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{5/2}\right\}
. Note the minimizers of
F
are
\boldsymbol{w}=\pm\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}
. We will show that
\boldsymbol{w}
is close to these minimizers: in particular, that
\min\left\{\left\|\boldsymbol{w}-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\right\|^{2},\left\|\boldsymbol{w}+\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\right\|^{2}\right\}\leq\varepsilon
.

Write
\boldsymbol{w}=c_{1}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+\cdots+c_{d}\boldsymbol{v}_{d}
. Thus, our goal is to show that
\left\lvert(c_{1}^{2}+\cdots+c_{d}^{2})-\lambda_{1}\right\rvert<\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. By
(
F.2
)
, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\right\|=\left\|\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{w}-\left\|\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\|=\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left((c_{1}^{2}+\cdots+c_{d}^{2})-\lambda_{i}\right)c_{i}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}\right\|.

That is, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{d}c_{i}^{2}\left((c_{1}^{2}+\cdots+c_{d}^{2})-\lambda_{i}\right)^{2}\leq\varepsilon^{2}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:expcagradimply1}

Furthermore by
(
F.2
)
, we have

\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})=(c_{1}^{2}+\cdots+c_{d}^{2})\boldsymbol{I}+2\sum_{i,j}c_{i}c_{j}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}^{\top}.

Since
\boldsymbol{w}
is a SOSP, for all
\boldsymbol{v}_{k},1\leq k\leq d
, we have

-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\leq\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{\top}\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{w})\boldsymbol{v}_{k}=(c_{1}^{2}+\cdots+c_{d}^{2})+2c_{k}^{2}-\lambda_{k}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:expcahessimply1}

We now break into cases:

•

Suppose for all
i
, we have
\left\lvert(c_{1}^{2}+\cdots+c_{d}^{2})-\lambda_{i}\right\rvert\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. From
(
F.2
)
, this gives
\sum_{i=1}^{d}c_{i}^{2}\leq\varepsilon
. Taking
k=1
in
(
F.2
)
, we obtain

-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\leq 3\sum_{i=1}^{d}c_{i}^{2}-\lambda_{1}\leq 3\varepsilon-\lambda_{1}\implies\lambda_{1}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}+3\varepsilon,

contradicting that
\varepsilon<\min\left\{1,\frac{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}}{16}\right\}
.

•

Else, suppose there exists
i
such that
\left\lvert(c_{1}^{2}+\cdots+c_{d}^{2})-\lambda_{i}\right\rvert<\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. Suppose that
i\geq 2
. Then taking
k=1
in
(
F.2
)
, we obtain

-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\leq\lambda_{i}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}+2c_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{1}\implies c_{1}^{2}\geq\frac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{i}}{2}-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\geq\frac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{4},

where the last inequality uses
\lambda_{i}\leq\lambda_{2}
and
\varepsilon<\left(\frac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{4}\right)^{2}
.

Note furthermore that as
\varepsilon\leq\left(\frac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{4}\right)^{2}
, as
\left\lvert(c_{1}^{2}+\cdots+c_{d}^{2})-\lambda_{i}\right\rvert<\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, and as
\lambda_{i}\leq\lambda_{2}<\lambda_{1}
, we have
\left\lvert(c_{1}^{2}+\cdots+c_{d}^{2})-\lambda_{1}\right\rvert>\frac{3(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})}{4}
. Thus
(
F.2
)
implies

\varepsilon^{2}>0+\frac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{4}\cdot\frac{9}{16}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2},

contradicting that
\varepsilon<\frac{3}{8}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{5/2}
.

Therefore, we must have
i=1
in the second case above. That is,
\left\lvert(c_{1}^{2}+\cdots+c_{d}^{2})-\lambda_{1}\right\rvert<\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, as desired.

Thus, it follows that running Perturbed GD or Restarted SGD as described in
Theorem
3.4
or
Theorem
3.5
respectively, we will obtain
\boldsymbol{w}
that is distance at most
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
from a global minimizer of
F
for
\varepsilon<\min\left\{1,\frac{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}}{16},\frac{3}{8}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{5/2}\right\}
. Here the number of oracle calls depends on
1/\varepsilon,d,F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
the same way as in
Theorem
3.4
or
Theorem
3.5
respectively. For
\varepsilon\geq\min\left\{1,\frac{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}}{16},\frac{3}{8}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{5/2}\right\}
, we can replace
\varepsilon
by any real strictly smaller than
\min\left\{1,\frac{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}}{16},\frac{3}{8}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{5/2}\right\}
in the guarantees from
Theorem
3.4
or
Theorem
3.5
.

Appendix G
Simulations

Our algorithmic results
Theorem
3.1
,
Theorem
3.2
,
Theorem
3.3
,
Theorem
3.4
, and
Theorem
3.5
have strong practical implications. They directly suggest that under generalized smoothness, the step sizes
\eta
that lead to convergence/successful optimization become smaller for larger initialization
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
and larger self-bounding functions
\rho_{1}(\cdot),\rho_{2}(\cdot)
. For example in
Theorem
3.1
, we set
\eta=\frac{1}{L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
where
L_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})=\max\left\{1,\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1),\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0}))\rho_{0}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1),\rho_{1}(F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})+1)\right\}
was defined in
(
3.2
)
.

That is, our work suggests that larger suboptimality at initialization and larger self-bounding functions shrink the ‘window’ for choosing a working
\eta
in practice, when the loss function satisfies generalized smoothness. This has strong practical implications: it implies that for losses with non-Lipschitz gradient/Hessian, one should tune
\eta
based on suboptimality at initialization. This contrasts sharply with the Lipschitz gradient/Hessian case, see e.g.
(
Bubeck et al., 2015
;
Jin et al., 2017
;
Fang et al., 2019
)
, where the range of working
\eta
is fixed in terms of the Lipschitz constant of the gradient and/or Hessian, and does not depend on the initialization.

In this section, we empirically validate this implication of our work.

G.1
Synthetic Simulations with GD

Simulation Details:

We consider
F(\boldsymbol{w})=\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{p}
for
p=2,3,4,5,6
, where
\boldsymbol{A}=\text{diag}\left(\frac{1}{20},\frac{1}{19},\ldots,\frac{1}{2},1\right)
. When
p=2
,
F(\boldsymbol{w})
is smooth. When
p\geq 3
,
F(\boldsymbol{w})
is not smooth, but it is straightforward to verify that it satisfies
Assumption
1.1
, similar to our verifications in
Subsection
A.2
. One can furthermore verify that as
p
increases, the corresponding self-bounding function
\rho_{1}(\cdot)
from
Assumption
1.1
increase. This choice of generalized smooth function was motivated by
Gaash et al. (2025)
, who used
\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{4}
with the exact same
\boldsymbol{A}
in their experiments to study optimization with first-order methods under generalized smoothness.

For each
p=2,3,4,5,6
, we consider the following settings for GD:

•

Step sizes: We consider 30 step sizes
\{\eta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{30},\eta_{1}<\cdots<\eta_{30}
evenly spaced on a log scale between
10^{-8}
and
10^{1}
, inclusive.

•

Initialization: For each step size
\eta_{i}
, we initialize GD at 4 distributions
\pi_{j}=\mathcal{N}(\vec{\boldsymbol{0}},c_{j}\boldsymbol{I}_{20})
for
c_{j}\in\{2.5,5,7.5,10\}
. For each of these 4 distributions
\pi_{j}
, we draw 100 points
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}\sim\pi_{j}
to use as our initialization.

•

Number of steps: For each
\eta_{i}
and each
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}\sim\pi_{j}
, we run GD initialized at
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}
with step size
\eta_{i}
for
T=1000
iterations. Here as
F
is known, we analytically compute the gradient.

For each
p
and initialization
\pi_{j}
, we consider all 30 possible
\eta_{i}
, which we plot on the
x
-axis. For each
\eta_{i}
, we consider all 100 initializations
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}\sim\pi_{j}
. For each initialization
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}
, letting
\{\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\}
be the resulting sequence of iterates of GD, we compute
\tfrac{\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}_{T})\right\|}{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
for
T=1000
. For
\eta_{i}
that led to faithful convergence of GD, on the
y
-axis, we then plot the mean of
\tfrac{\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}_{T})\right\|}{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
over those 100 initializations as a blue dot, with blue vertical error bars indicating
\pm 2
standard deviations. We considered the ratio
\tfrac{\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}_{T})\right\|}{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
because for
L
-smooth functions, established optimization theory predicts that this converges at a rate independent of
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
and only depending on
T
and
L

(
Bubeck et al., 2015
)
.

The simulations for
Subsection
G.1
were run on a Jupyter notebook in Python in Google Colab Pro, connected to a single NVIDIA T4 GPU. Our code can be found in the attached files.

Divergence of GD and working step sizes:

We observe that for some
\eta_{i}
larger than some threshold depending on
p
and
\pi_{j}
, the iterates of GD diverge. In particular, the resulting ratio
\tfrac{\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}_{T})\right\|}{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
becomes massive, often on the order of
10^{5}
or more, indicating that
\eta_{i}
was too large for GD to converge. To identify the smallest
\eta_{i}
where this first occurs, or equivalently find the largest working step size among
\{\eta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{30}
, for a given
\pi_{j}
and
\eta_{i}
, we computed the average
\tfrac{\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}_{T})\right\|}{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
over the 100 initializations. If this average was 100 or more times larger than this average for
\eta_{i-1}
, we took this as an indication that the iterates of GD with this step size
\eta_{i}
or larger step sizes diverge, and for this
p
and
\pi_{j}
, we stopped considering any larger
\eta_{i^{\prime}}
,
i^{\prime}>i
. We then save this
\eta_{i}
to indicate the smallest
\eta_{i}
for which divergence occurred. This
\eta_{i}
is indicated with a red line in the following plots.

This smallest
\eta_{i}
for which divergence occurred plays a crucial role in validating our theoretical claims. Established optimization theory predicts that for smooth functions (here, when
p=2
), this
\eta_{i}
is identical across different initializations
(
Bubeck et al., 2015
)
. Meanwhile for generalized smooth functions, as per our remarks earlier and from
Subsection
3.6
, we predict that as
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
increases, the range of working step sizes, and consequently also the smallest
\eta_{i}
for which divergence occurs, will
decrease
. Note as
c_{j}
increases (recall
\pi_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(\vec{\boldsymbol{0}},c_{j}\boldsymbol{I}_{20})
and
c_{j}\in\{2.5,5,7.5,10\}
), we expect
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
to increase, at least on average or with high probability over the 100 initializations
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}\sim\pi_{j}
.

Results:

Our simulations validate this theory very accurately. Note in the following figures that the
y
-axis is normalized, as we plot
\tfrac{\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}_{T})\right\|}{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
where
T=1000
. Thus larger
c_{j}
lead to comparable values on the
y
-axis.

•

When
p=2
: In
Figure
1
, we plot the results in the manner described above for all 4 initializations
\pi_{j}
. As is predicted by established optimization theory for smooth functions
(
Bubeck et al., 2015
)
, the first step size leading to divergence
\eta_{i}
is identical across all the
\pi_{j}
.

•

When
p=3,4,5,6
: We plot the results in the manner described above for all 4 initializations
\pi_{j}
in
Figure
2
,
Figure
3
,
Figure
4
,
Figure
5
respectively. Unlike the
p=2
case, in all of these cases, the first step size leading to divergence
\eta_{i}
generally decreases as the covariance
c_{j}\boldsymbol{I}_{20}
of
\pi_{j}
increases from
2.5
to 10.

We also notice the following, both in line with our theoretical claims:

•

For a given
p
, consider how this first step size
\eta_{i}
leading to divergence decreases as the covariance
c_{j}\boldsymbol{I}_{20}
of
\pi_{j}
increases from
2.5
to 10. We find that the rate of this decrease increases as
p
increases. The ratio of the first
\eta_{i}
leading to divergence for
\pi_{1}
vs
\pi_{4}
is approximately
4.18,4.18,8.53,17.43
for
p=3,4,5,6
respectively.

As remarked earlier, for larger
p
, the corresponding self-bounding function
\rho_{1}(\cdot)
is larger for
F(\boldsymbol{w})=\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{w}\right\|^{p}
(see
Subsection
A.2
for a similar verification). Thus this behavior is consistent with our results, as the step size from all of our results depends on
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
through
\rho_{1}(\cdot)
.

•

Fixing
\pi_{j}
and comparing across
p
, we see that the first step size leading to divergence
\eta_{i}
decreases as
p
increases. Again this is not a surprise considering our theoretical results, as for larger
p
, both
F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})
for
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}\sim\pi_{j}
and the self-bounding function
\rho_{1}(\cdot)
become larger.

For each
p\in\{2,3,4,5,6\}
and
\pi_{j}
, we also record the smallest
\eta_{i}
for which divergence occurred in Table

on page

, which highlights the aforementioned trends.

G.2
Synthetic Simulations with SGD

Simulation Details:

We adopt the exact same settings as in
Subsection
G.1
. The only difference is that we study SGD rather than GD, and hence we simulate stochastic gradients. We do so similarly to
Gaash et al. (2025)
: we artificially add
\mathcal{N}\left(\vec{\boldsymbol{0}},0.01\boldsymbol{I}_{20}\right)
to
\nabla F
at each iteration of SGD.
12
12

12

Note our result for convergence of SGD to FOSPs,
Theorem
3.3
, applies for Gaussian noise as per
Remark
7
.
The simulations for
Subsection
G.2
were again run on a Jupyter notebook in Python in Google Colab Pro, connected to a single NVIDIA T4 GPU. Our code is in the attached files.

Results:

Our conclusions are similar to those from
Subsection
G.1
. When
p=2
, as predicted by established optimization theory for smooth functions, the first step size leading to divergence
\eta_{i}
is identical across the
\pi_{j}
(see
Figure
6
). In contrast for
p=3,4,5,6
, this
\eta_{i}
generally decreases as the covariance
c_{j}\boldsymbol{I}_{20}
of
\pi_{j}
increases from
2.5
to 10 (see
Figure
7
,
Figure
8
,
Figure
9
,
Figure
10
). We note that while the general trends are similar to those from
Subsection
G.1
, we can clearly see the presence of the stochastic gradients in these plots. In many of these plots,
\tfrac{\left\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w}_{T})\right\|}{F(\boldsymbol{w}_{0})}
becomes roughly constant for
\eta
large enough such that
T=1000
yields reasonable convergence; for such
\eta
, by
T=1000
, the true gradients are small enough and the noise from the stochastic gradients takes over.

Once more, consider how the first step size leading to divergence
\eta_{i}
decreases as the covariance
c_{j}\boldsymbol{I}_{20}
of
\pi_{j}
increases from
2.5
to 10. We find that the rate of this decrease generally increases as
p
increases. We also again see that fixing
\pi_{j}
and comparing across
p
, the first step size leading to divergence
\eta_{i}
decreases as
p
increases. As discussed in
Subsection
G.1
, both of these phenomena are consistent with our theoretical results.
For each
p\in\{2,3,4,5,6\}
and
\pi_{j}
, we again record the smallest
\eta_{i}

on page

.
